## G: go home with you–grazing on

### go home with you
 
 <i>exclam.</i> a catchphrase indicating disbelief or impatience (? a var. of go on (with you)). [ca1951 (Wellington) Aw, go home with you - you can't tell me that]

### go hostile (at)
 
 <i>phr.</i> become angry (with), go off (at), express annoyance (at).

>  <b>P;ARTRIDGE</b> When I done all my sugar and never even had the makings, he went very hostile because I never told him I was swept. .. Well, you have had a fair go behind the line and it's just about time you turned hostile.. Hey, turn it up and stop all those gloomy forecasts or you will find that I can go very hostile, and it's time we all took a tumble to ourselves and discussed something pleasant, say a little party. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Hostile. often pronounced horstile. Angry, annoyed; esp go hostile, to get angry: Australian and New Zealand military: in G.W. -and after. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,408</i>)

>  <b>HADYN</b> Wouldn't he go hostile if he knew who pinched his bacon <i>(1941,NZ Soldiers in England,17</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> All went well until ... a special goods train was met on the single main line ... The driver of the special was hostile and rightly maintained that he had the right of road. <i>(1951,Confessions,60</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Long Tom ... [66] went hostile, as did his junior assistant when for an hour they failed to get the patient [a pig] in the pen ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,65</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I'll see if I can get the dog for you and if your missus goes hostile about it I'll take it myself. <i>(1962,One of Us,51</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> That afternoon the troops again went hostile, the grub was worse, just plain stinking. <i>(1978,Soldier Country,17</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> go hostile act angrily; eg `If he says one more word to her, I'll really go hostile.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to be sown or prepared for harvest.

>  Considerable areas of silage going in, many for the first time. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  Twenty-four hectares of rape and turnips which would have been sown in wheat in August will instead go in to matua prairie grass and pawera red clover. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,8</i>)

### go it
 
 <i>v.</i> to argue

>  <b>SARGESON</b> going: going it, arguing; get going about, started talking volubly about. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to]

### go like a marble clock
 
 <i>phr.</i> run or go well

>  `Morning boss. Still raining. How are the Russkies?' `Going like a marble clock, Smithy' `Good on them.' <i>(Jun 1973,Meanjin Quarterly,32/2/133</i>) [NOTES: (D. Davin)]

### go like a two-bob watch
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go swimmingly (cf. go like a train) [also go off like ..., of a woman to become sexually responsive [AND 1971]]

>  Have heard the expression occasionally <i>(Sep 21, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### go mad-dog
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> For three days she laid the table and took the mending basket on to the verandah in the afternoon but after that she `went mad-dog again' as her father expressed it and there was no holding her. <i>(1916,Aloe, The,75,1982 O'Sullivan ed.</i>)

### go mates with
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Brown lost no time in making a contract to 'go mates' with another digger. <i>(1880,Tales of the Goldfields,59</i>)

>  <b>LAPHAM in DAVIN</b> At this time I was mates with a young fellow called Jim Smith, a good enough lad as a mate, and would do just as big a day's labour as any man. <i>(1953,NZ Short Stories,57</i>) [NOTES: Original quote from 1880]

>  I will accept his proposal to go mates with him. <i>(Mar 1890,Good Words,211/1</i>)

### go nap on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to bet on as a favourite.

>  He [the horse] had a lot in his favor [sic], and those who were aware of this went `nap' on the son of Birkenhead, but second with no dividend was the best he could do. <i>(Nov 5, 1910,NZ Truth,280/2/4</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> to fall for, lose one's head over

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> `You know, Mark, you tough wiry chaps are all a bit hot-tempered, that's why the girls all go nap on you the way they do'. I hadn't noticed it ... I decided to keep my eyes open, the next girl talked to. <i>(1924,Me and Gus,11,1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm ed.</i>)

### go off
 
 <i>v.</i> lose one's temper, go berserk, riot

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Every New Year's Eve in prison is a time for great revelry and excitement. Guys get druink if they can, and at midnight, all over the country, the men in prisons start 'going off'.<i>Ibid.</i> 248 GO OFF (AT) (v) Lose one's temper, go berserk, riot. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,212</i>)

### go off at
 
 <i>v.</i> To lose one's temper with, to reprimand, to scold fiercely [ca1935]

>  <b>McGILL</b> go off at to abuse, eg `If she says any more about me behind my back, you have my permission to go off at her hard as you like.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go off fat
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [prime stock or fat milk-lambs] to leave a farm for slaughter at freezing works.

>  All lambs go off fat. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1480</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The progeny of the Down cross make the primest lambs, but they have one disadvantage, that unless they go off fat the first season, they are more difficult to winter as hoggets than a long-woolled cross. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,68</i>)

>  All lambs go off fat, of course. <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,57</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Lambs went off fat to the works while the talk around about was of hogget ill thrift. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,197</i>)

### go off like a packet of crackers
 
 <i>phr.</i> to explode with anger [ca1920 (Auckland) R Mason]

>  <b>UPFIELD</b> <i>(1937,Murder Down Under,46,Penguin ed.</i>)

### go off pop
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry (foll. by at: with a person or thing; scold etc.)

>  <b>CADEY</b> There's no need to go off pop quite like that. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,126</i>)

>  [The editor] went off pop at the premeditated insult. <i>(Sep 16, 1905,NZ Truth,13-1-7</i>)

>  A Ballarat magistrate has gone off pop, saying he was insulted by a lawyer nodding his head at him. It is a bit risky nodding at some magistrates, especially outside pubs. <i>(Sep 12, 1908,NZ Truth,169/1/5</i>)

>  The announcement made on Saturday that the local beds would remain closed until May, 1911, and that the public would have to depend solely on the Stewart Island and Bluff supplies naturally caused an unthinking section of the community to `go off pop,' and they were promptly bolstered up by the restauranteurs, . . . <i>(Apr 30, 1910,NZ Truth,253/4/5</i>)

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> There's no need to go off pop like that. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,127</i>)

### go on to the land
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to take up farming.

>  <b>MENZIES</b> So it is not hard to imagine the limited horizons of the young people of the second generation reared in the Bay. The boys went on to the land; and two of the girls married cadets. <i>(1970,Story of Menzies Bay, The,12</i>)

### go out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [rams] to be moved to ewes for tupping.

>  The rams go out after shearing in early March. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  .. it is important to have ewes in good body condition (CS 3-4) by the time mating begins and preferably on good leafy pasture food as the rams go out. <i>(1994,Guide to Feed Planning for Sheep Farmers,38</i>)

>  Our rams go out March 20 so we're not chasing the early lamb market. <i>(Jul 2001,Country-Wide,11</i>)

### go out of
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to give up an aspect of farming.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Since George's father went out of cows in 1928, the Moore operation has been intensive grain and seed cropping ... <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,49</i>)

### go outlaw
 
 <i>phr.</i> of an animal, go wild ?

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... many strange and exciting stories of the area were told me by my since departed friend, Fred Simmons, or Fossicking Fred, as he was affectionately called by his workmates, on account of his ability to trace their hideouts, and either bring in or destroy wild or domestic animals that had gone outlaw. <i>Ibid.</i> 111 One station holder, after several attempts had been made to muster some hundreds of domestic cattle that had gone outlaw, offered [pound]1 a head for them, to anyone delivering them at the Station yards. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,110</i>)

### go over
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Some of us had added to our boots what is known as a `counter'. It is a stiff piece of leather, which, commencing at the instep, runs round the back of the boot, above the heel proper, acts as an ankle support, and prevents its wearer from `going over'. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,14</i>)

### go over the hill
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be sent to Lyttelton Gaol

>  <b>TLS</b> Going over the hill for the umpteenth time on the same old charge. <i>(1936,People in Prison,211</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> SPC Silverstream: a catchphrase indicating the person referred to is `crazy' and should go `over the hill' to Porirua Mental Hospital [ca 1941]

### go overland
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to shear a sheep up 'in the branches', or far from its skin [especially if it is wet or cotty].

>  <b>MILLS</b> It is almost impossible to shear these sheep close to the skin the first time so a shearer goes 'overland' and then goes back for what wool is left. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,99</i>)

### go piggy
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [sheep and cattle] to lie stationary when under threat.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> piggy, to go: when they are pressed beyond their physical endurance and feel unable to escape, sheep will go piggy. They will collapse and remain on the ground until convinced that no tormentor, such as impatient shepherd (even killer keas will send them piggy) is about. Then, but only then will they rise and struggle valiantly on. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> Rod stood in a gateway while the drover clumsily tried to cut the weaners through. But they soon turned piggy and he was getting nowhere with them. [piggy, to turn] <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,231</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> [The Merinos] would just lie down and refuse to move; go `piggy' as we called it. <i>Ibid.</i> 153 The sheep quickly went `piggy' in the heat, and had to be allowed to pick their way, feet at a time, up the sandy tracks.... <i>(1971,Sunrise,88</i>)

### go psycho
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become violent

>  Unfortunately, the colloquial term to "go psycho" is all too easily confused with psychosis, and sensational media reporting of rare, tragic cases further reinfocres the link in the public mind between mental illness and violence. <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### go ram's horn
 
 <i>phr.</i> = go butcher's hook

>  G.E.J.: Can any `Kia Ora Coo-ee-ite' dig up anything to beat this for the pure and unadulterated? A certain New Zealand Regiment, camped on the Jordan flats, recently came under the eagle eye of brother `Jacko', who immediately went `butcher's hook' or `ram's horn' and launched forth much frightfulness by lugging a 5.9 up on to one of the spare hills and chucking ironmongery promiscuously about the landscape. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee</i>) [NOTES: 1918 Aug 15 (Cairo) Kia-ora Coo-ee (1981) Second Series 2/5]

### go round
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to look around stock, especially ewes at lambing time. (See [ride round; look; look round](../R#ride-round;-look;-look-round))

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Went round ewes and looked up the working bullock. <i>(Aug 6, 1914,Diary</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> H Cooley went round the sheep & found a ewe logged up which he released. <i>(Oct 7, 1914,Diary</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> As the lambing period approaches the ewes should be gone round regularly to assist any that are cast ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,74</i>)

### go rude at
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> go rude at: to exercise the fine art of the INSULT with respect to someone <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,47</i>)

### go south
 
 <i>phr.</i> go to the Antarctic

>  The seal research proigramme will be continued by Mr. D. Greenwood, who is going south for his third season. <i>(1970,Antarctic,5(11): 448</i>)

### go swag on back
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  So both of them packed up, and said they would go "swag on back," and have a look at the country, at the same time they would be working their way to Canterbury. <i>(Feb 10, 1875,Marlborough Express,5</i>)

### go through
 
 <i>v.</i> to search pockets, to rob, to roll. [criminals', ?obs]

>  Maggie McNeil, alias Linton, and Annie Maud Sales, ..., made their second bow before Magistrate Widdowson, ..., to answer the more serious charge of having `gone through' Otto Hansen for his pocket-book, ... <i>(Dec 18, 1909,NZ Truth,234/6/6</i>)

>  He intended to give the money to Dagg, but on his way back he was drugged in a hotel and was `gone through' for all the money he had. <i>(Feb 19, 1910,NZ Truth,243/7/2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> to abscond, decamp: Australian confidence tricksters' since ca. 1920. Baker, 1945. perhaps to go through a place (pillage it) and pass rapidly on. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,295</i>)

### go tipi haere
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go walkabout [1987 Feb 18 p.c. Rachael Marr I went tipi haere]

### go to hash
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be made useless, to go to pot

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Is hall our work of the las' two months to go to 'ash. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,132</i>)

### go to market
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of a horse) to buck

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Go to market:</i> of a horse bucking <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> become angry or upset (cf. go to town about something) [ca1941 Havelock, Wellington]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> About 8 p.m. he'd arise from off the sofa and go to market because the poor woman hadn't cooked a hot tea for him. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,17</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> ... go to market. [check quot] <i>(1936,Follow the Call,17</i>)

>  <i>market ... 3 go to market NZ & Austral. colloq. </i>become angry, behave in an angry manner. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,460</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) expressing exasperation or anger; go to blazes

### go to pot
 
 <i>phr.</i> go to hell, to become angry, to go off pop [also found in general English sense to go to the devil; to become run down] - [known from 1930s]

>  <b>SKEY</b> Loiter not, lest Buchanan go to pot. <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,178</i>)

### go to the bone
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to draft stock liberally.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Go to the bone or to the Doctor: To draft out everything which will grade fit for export. And very often one or two that won't. [go to the doctor] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,36</i>)

### go to the glass
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  But, alack and alas, Eady has once more gone to the glass. His miraculous `cure' has been exploded like a pricked balloon, and great was the noise and fall thereof. <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205 /7/1</i>)

### go to the mat
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>KOHERE</b> Many a Te Aute boy has been said to have "gone to the mat," or to have answered "the call of the wilds," because, after receiving a college education, he has gone back to the pa, making little or no use of his education. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,102</i>)

### go to the pack
 
 <i>phr.</i> to deteriorate, go to the dogs, go to pieces (of a thing or person)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Some saying it was awful how a man with connections in the Old Country could go to the pack like this. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,75</i>) [NOTES: Page 67 in the 1963(1976) edition; chapter 14 : Night of Dec. 24]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pack, go to the</i>. To go to pieces (fig.); lose a leading position: s.> coll.: New Zealand: C. 20. Perhaps ex a trained or a domestic animal going wild, or ex a dog falling back into the pack. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,598</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Gone to the pack.</i> A New Zealand coll. variant (C. 20) of <i>gone to the dogs. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> [We] went to his hippah upon an island ... the Schooner could proceed no further than a Pah called Couranghie ... <i>(1820,Journal,18,1928 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [McCrae uses HIPPAH only the once: ? from written accounts, using Pah subsequent to his contact with missionaries and Maoris]]

>  [The traveller to New Zealand] might be ... told that things had `gone to the pack,' .... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: Mcnab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... go to the pack. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,75</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the rejection of it [a book] knocked me back completely. I went to the pack and drifted around the country from job to job. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,39</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i> go to the pack</i> to deteriorate; eg `Since his wife left him for his best mate, Reg has gone rapidly to the pack.' NZA. - to fail persistently, as recorded in Barry Crump's <i>A Good Keen Man</i>, 1960. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go to the pub, Dad
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> go to the pub, Dad! contemptuous teen advice to an adult being square; ref. Partridge A Dictionary of Catch Phrases; c. 1970. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go to town
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry or excited, to go to market; to play up [ca1945 used often in Wellington, and in Marlborough e.g. He went to town when he saw the mess. I'll go to town on him if he does it again]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [The outlaw horse] went to town so determinedly that he never even got into the saddle. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,69</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> [The outlaw horse] went to town so determinedly that he never even got into the saddle. <i>(1961,Short Stories (various authors),179</i>) [NOTES: 1953 in 1961 Reid Short Stories 179]

>  town ... 7. go to town. ... b. Austral. & N.Z. inf. to lose one's temper <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1239</i>) [NOTES: [1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 508]]

### go to vats
 
 <i>phr.</i> [freezing works]

>  <b>McDONALD</b> For many years hearts and livers had a very poor market, and so nearly all went `to vats', that is, were boiled down for their fat and manurial value. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

### go under
 
 <i>v.</i> To be convicted [criminals')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> GO UNDER (v) To be convicted. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### go up
 
 <i>v.</i> to travel to mainland NZ [(Chatham Islands)]

>  Chatham Island term for `going over to New Zealand'. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  *Go up, to.- Chatham Island term for `going over to New Zealand.' <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

### go up as high as Baldwin
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go for a skate; to go up sky-high

>  <b>SCOTT</b> You wait till you come before the stewards; we don't stand no shenannikin. It's for crooked running...and you'll go up as high as Baldwin <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,34</i>)

>  <b>ALLISON</b> All countries have their slang expressions, and New Zealand is no exception to the rule. `To go up for a skate' is one colonial idiom, and `To go up as high as [index to common names 283-293], baldfish 598-90 ' is another. Both colloquialisms are similar in meaning, but `To go up as high as Baldwin' had its origin in the 90's, after a visit to this country of the balloonist, Professor Baldwin. <i>Ibid.</i> 68 ... Poor Captain Lorraine [a balloonist killed in an accident]. He met with disaster. To use a colloqual expression `He went up as high as Baldwin'. <i>(1946,New Zealander Looks Back, A,63</i>) [NOTES: [c1890's]]

### go up the spout
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <i>going up the neck</i>: This expression refers to the blows on a sheep from the brisket towards the head. `Opening up the neck' or `going up the spout' mean the same thing. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### go vegetable
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> vegetable go to sleep or look dazed; street talk; eg `Foss goes vegetable on us all the time these days.' [Alison Gray] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,119</i>)

### go walkabout
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BRAMLEY</b> ... our two-year-old Jersey bull had gone walkabout on the beach ... <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,29</i>)

### go Westward Ho
 
 <i>phr.</i> an occas. elaboration of <i>go west</i>, to die; to come to grief

>  <b>SCANLON</b> Poor old Tom went Westward Ho last night. <i>(1926,Forgotten Men,6</i>)

### go, a
 
 <i>exclam.</i> Truce ! Pax! (? Is this from cribbage.)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> I gave Sam a good dusting .... and Sam suddenly cried `a go'. <i>(1948,There's Gold in Them Hills,32</i>)

### go-ashore
 
 <i>n.</i> Various sailors anglicisations or re-formations of Maori proper (or place) names, probably in contexts of English goashore, go on shore; given probably as nicknames of people. (For possible phonology, see GOASHORE n2.

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Tarra, besides his head wife who was nearly as old as himself, had got another in the full bloom of youth, and extremely [210] handsome. This lady, whose face was familiar to all the English sailors that happened to touch here [North Auckland: ?`there' check] went by one of those arbitrary names which they are [check were] so fond of assigning to women of her character; and was called by them Mrs. Goshore, [check itals: Mrs.] a corruption, it would seem, of the words go on shore, [check whether itals or quotes] and originating, perhaps, in some alluring request to that purpose on the part of the frail one herself. [North Auckland] <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/209</i>)

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> The name of the Native that killed the man was called goashore [Cook Strait]. <i>(1834,Autobiography,27</i>)

>  A small settlement belonging to Te Waukau (alias Go-ashore) <i>(1842,NZ Journal,3/73/259</i>) [NOTES: Tuckett (Nelson, Motueka)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> One specimen [Cook Medal] ... was found at Pelorous [sic] Sound, in an old go-ashore or three-legged iron pot ... <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,211</i>)

>  Mr Shepherd.. bought a large tract of eligible land.. for two check shirts and an iron pot, or go-ashore, as it is called by the natives. <i>(1839,New Zealand in 1839,34</i>)

### go-forward
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby term

>  They've now got more players capable of giving them go-forward than last year. <i>(Jul 15, 2004,Dominion Post,D12</i>)

>  His go-forward is exceptional... There's no go-forward. They're standing flat. <i>(Aug 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  ...their No 8's pretty good and we tried to take it to them stop them getting their go-forward so that we could get ours and get the backs going. <i>(Jun 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C16</i>)

>  When the forwards get the go-forward ball, my job is just to get the ball in his hands and let him do his magic. <i>(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### go-in
 
 <i>n.</i> [?Used in NZ. -->1951 16+ M 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30B GO-IN [a fight]]

### go-slow
 
 <i>n.</i> detention unit

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Jake punched [the screw] in the mouth. So they chucked Jake down the go-slow for a week. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 GO-SLOW (n) Detention unit. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,88</i>)

### go-to-Jesus
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of clothing) formal, suitable for wearing to church (cf. ODE go-to-meeting adj. dated)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,47,1944 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [? omit]]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> go-to-Jesus tie: bow tie. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

### go/be sent down the road
 
 <i>phr.</i> (occas. down the track) to be sacked [ca1941 Havelock He should be given sixpence and his swag and sent down the road; for Feilding only, go/be sent under the subway (there is a subway close to the work through which men pass to get to or away from the works)]

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Remote stations usually have labour problems, and I couldn't imagine the man in question going down the road because of his language. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,99</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> down the road where sacked people are sent. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,39</i>)

>  <b>MISS J. HALL</b> To be sent down the road .... Expressions heard used in Central Otago and Fiord country Otago, by people engaged in gold-mining or bush work. In the beginning, I should imagine, it was a literal description of what happened to the man - since there was only one road leading out of the community ... It is now used in isolated communities to describe someone being dismissed, even if he can get another job that keeps him in the community. <i>(May 1951,Letter</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> boot: can be used for the things you hike in, and there is an expression `to put the boot in' which means metaphorically to kick someone, as `then he really put the boot in and sent him down the road' (fired him); or gave him the boot. However, the commonest use of boot refers to what you would call the TRUNK of your car. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 sent down the road: To be relieved of the necessity of returning to your place of employment as a result of involuntary separation from your employer. FIRED. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,16</i>) [NOTES: cf P. get the road: be dismissed from employment: Glasgow, (-1934) Prob. suggested by walking-orders.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Never leaving a bloke alone. So I got on my bike and went down the road. <i>Ibid.</i> 91 You'll go down the road if ya don't wake ya ideas up, I said. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,21</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> [in reference to the above] I'm uncertain of the meaning of `You'll go down the road' <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,121</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> By God, he'd keep a close eye on Master Hickey and at the first opportunity, the first infraction, he'd be sent down the road so fast he'd wonder what hit him. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,85</i>)

>  But it didn't compensate for the short weeks when wages sank toward the $233.75 minimum, or the slack period when workers went down the road. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,82</i>)

### goa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kowhai](kiw-kwi.md#kowhai))

### goad-boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IRELAND</b> During this time he worked for his tucker on a bullock team, as a goad-boy. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,26</i>)

### Goashore
 
 <i>n.</i> whalers' alteration of a Maori place name on Banks Peninsula (?influenced by popularity of goashore n2.)

>  found it to be two Mowries from Bloody Jack, who was in Oashore Bay with fifteen boats. [ed note Glossary 162 ... probably this bight, and the now-called `Horseshoe Bay', were originally named `Go-ashore' p157 Goashore: Whaler's name for a camp-oven. [in Notes and Corrections to Glossary: cites Kohua as M. name and Goashore as a whaler's pron. <i>(Oct 31, 1938,Piraki Log,102</i>)

>  [Table : re Akaroa] Occupation Piraki .. .. Whaling station .. Oishew .. .. Whaling station .. [Oioha] Rouncataki .. Squatters .. <i>(Sep 17, 1840,GBPP,1841 (No.311) 82</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Sep 17 Commander Stanley (`Britomart') to Hobson in GBPP House of Commons]

>  [usually here Oashore, Orsha = Oahoa Bay] in the afternoon went to Go'ashore in the boat. [Oahoa Bay s. from Piraki] <i>Ibid.</o> 126 Brown from Orsha came and took off his bellows, nail-mould, and a loan of an anvil. <i>(Apr 12, 1841,Piraki Log,123</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [=Oihoa] Cattle have been driven from Oihoa (or Go-ashore), ... to Akaroa in about eight hours; ... my informant was one of the drovers. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,343</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Nov Capt. Smith's report in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 343]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> .. of the facility of entering on the sea shore (near Goashore) [Sumner] [sic] I cannot speak. <i>(Apr 6, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 6 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 205]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> West of Akaroa harbour, there are two or three whaling stations on the coast - Hikurangi, Pirangi, and Oihoa (Go-ashore). <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,337</i>)

>  [=Oihowa Bay] Left Goashore at 6 a.m. and commenced arranging our packs. <i>(Mar 1, 1849,Torlesse Papers,63,1958 ed.</i>)

### goashore
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. Maori oven 2. A vessel used for boiling food by means of heated stones, a boiler; related to Maori kohu, kokohu `hollow'. [The word chiefly used in whaling or sailing contexts. (hist.)]

>  <b>COLLINS</b> T'souduckey [on Tuki's chart] A three-legged iron pot for boiling. The word is probably a re-formation of Maori koohua, attracted to the forms of English collocations go-ashore etc. The phonology of koohua > go(a)shore can be explained: Maori /k/ perceived as a [g] realisation Maori /o:/ perceived as a [ou] Maori /h/ realised in tribal dialect as [ ] and perceived as /ua/ perceived as See the spelling goahore, an intermediate form. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,344</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A pot Koeshooa <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/334</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Natives now begin to use Iron pots, known to them by the name of, `Go on Shores,' from the circumstance of Boats going up and down the Rivers, coming to an Anchor and going on Shore to Cook, but the Natives prefer the Old plan [sc coppre Mouries, hangis] and I think they are right. [fn ed.] Elsewhere Markham uses the more usual `go ashore', probably a corruption of the Maori `kohua', a vessel for boiling food by means of heated stones. <i>Ibid.</i> 57 We went to Showracky to get some more `Go ashores' or Iron pots, for Household purposes, tin pannikins to drink out of. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),45,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LANG</b> note [reference to arbitrary names given people by the Maoris]. The New Zealanders, for example, observing that the sailors on board the whaling vessels that touched at their ports uniformly carried an iron pot with them to cook their provisions when they went on shore, conceived the words Go ashore, which they were accustomed to hear when preparations were making for landing, was the proper name for an iron pot and they have accordingly been adopted as such in the New Zealand [Maori] language. <i>(1839,New Zealand (various authors/titles),15</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Lang NZ in 1839 Letters 15]

>  (Journal John Pierce) Soon a fire was made, ... and the go-ashore, or three-legged iron pot, full of pigeons. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,19/245</i>)

>  [The Maoris] boil the [shark] in their `go shores', or small iron pots. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,92/177</i>)

>  <b>BURNS</b> [Vocabulary of English and NZ Words] A pot Koeshooa <i>(1844,NZ Chief,23</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> In the interior of the Pa the Wahines[e-acute], or matrons, are busy weaving flax-mats, cleaning potatoes or fish, or engaged in the superintendence of a Maori oven, or a huge gipsy-looking cauldron, called a `go-ashore', and can only afford to greet the visitor with a whining `tena koe pakeha', sighing as though they were very much to be pitied. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,160</i>)

>  The bone having been doubled at the hock so that it might be put into the pot, or as they call it, goahore <i>(Feb 19, 1852,Australian & NZ Gazette,62/528</i>) [NOTES: Journal Geo. Rich Tauranga, Poverty Bay]

>  ... he put on the `go ashore' <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,119</i>)

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> (Otago back-block run) Our domestic economy was at first unique, the cooking gear comprised a couple of three-legged iron pots called go-ashore's, ditto tin cans named billy's, a frying pan and a circular tin vessel called a camp oven .... pot lids serving as plates and dishes. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> At another time, having a store up the country, the [trade-talk] would be of blankets, black silk ribbon, print, and calico, of tobacco, `go a shores', [159] sugar, and spades. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,158</i>)

>  <b>AN OLD COLONIST</b> A large go-ashore, or three-legged pot, of the size and shape of the cauldron usually introduced in the witch scene in Macbeth. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,124</i>)

>  <b>INNES</b> [The camp-oven] was a large iron pot ... The lid was flat and covered with hot ashes; there was another pot used called by the euphonious name of `go a shore', which used to hang by a chain over the fire this was used for boiling. [ca 1850-1851] <i>(1879,Canterbury Sketches,23</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Our potatoes, instead of being turned out clean and mealy from a Maori oven, were boiled and sodden from an iron pot, or goashore, as it is called [F.N.] An iron pot so called because it was always taken on shore from the boat to cook food [Manawatu 1862.] <i>(1880,Travels in NZ,175</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Go-ashore, ... an iron pot or cauldron, with three iron feet, and two ears, from which it was suspended by a wire handle over the fire. It is a corruption of the Maori word kohua (q.v.), by the law of Hobson-Jobson. [1849 Power, 1877 Old Col, 1879 Innes] <i>(1898,Austral English,163</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> And the last day of all he [sc the first white man] gave Toia another pot, and speaking vehemently, with much pointing at it, said: `Now go ashore.' So we took that to be its name; for do we not call it this distant day a `kohua?' (corruption of `go ashore'). <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,72</i>)

>  Kohua was the Maori name for an iron cooking-pot; `goashore', the whalers' nearest attempt to pronounce it (Canon Stack) Or vice versa (?Anson) <i>(1911,Piraki Log</i>) [NOTES: Glossary corrections]

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [reference Wellington 1850s] We got a `fiery stick' from some Maoris ... who were waiting for a `Goashore' of `Ripiri' to cool ... The mess was in time cool enough, when they all surrounded the great pot, some with pewter spoons, ... but most with shells from the beach ... . <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,18</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 18]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> [In the primitive homestead of the 'eighties] Camp ovens, go-ashores, and billies stood on the floor, or were slung from bars above the empty hearth. <i>(1926,Tutira,150</i>)

>  go-ashore n. an iron cauludron or pot with three feet and two ears, by which it is suspended from a wire handle over a fire. [corrupt. fr. Maori kohua, oven, <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>BEASLEY</b> Go-ashore: This [common utensil] was a round kettle-like pot of considerable dimensions with legs like a camp oven and with a handle to enable it to be hung over the fire. This was commonly called a `go-ashore'. Just how it got its name is not quite clear but the accepted explanation is as follows:- Early in the `white' history of New Zealand, some Maoris were anxious to know the name of the utensil. The white man addressed was not so much concerned about the enquiry as about landing, so instructed his companies to `go ashore'. Mistaking the instruction for an answer to their question the Maoris applied the name `goashore' to this particular kind of kettle. Whatever the origin of the name, the `go-ashore' was a valued part of the early settlers' equipment. <i>(1936,Pioneering Days,35</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> go-ashore. `An iron pot or cauldron, with three iron-feet, and two ears, from which it was suspended by a wire handle over the fire,' Morris: New Zealand coll. (- 1849) >, by 1880, S.E. Ex Maori kohua by `Hobson-Jobson'. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,335</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> go-ashore. `An iron pot or cauldron, with three iron-feet, and two ears, from which it was suspended by a wire handle over the fire,' Morris: New Zealand coll. (- 1849) >, by 1880, S.E. Ex Maori kohua by `Hobson-Jobson'. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,335</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] `go-ashore,' a cauldron (kohua), `taihoa,' and `goory,' a dog (kuri). <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  go-ashore: probably corruption of kohua; three-legged iron pot. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  go-ashore n. three-legged iron pot, carried by whalers as they `go ashore' [Poss. corr. of a Maori word]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> For although they [sc. the women on Stewart Island in 1872] had a camp oven, a `go-ashore' or witch's pot, the round pot on legs ... their homes required much else. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,113</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... Cook ... interfered and asked Te Hira why he was tahae-ing the goahore. He meant `taking away the pot', but in his imperfect knowledge of the language he used the word stealing. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/7/121</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> A young tender thing who should have been set in a gilded house, ... instead of lugging the huge goashore pots [21] from the cooking-trench, baking, washing, mending, bearing progeny to Peregrine Lovel. <i>(1938,Promenade,20</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1955 R.Mason notes that she would use the word currently. Her family have a small china ornament the shape of a goashore which is always referred to in the family as `The Goashore'.]

### goat boat
 
 <i>n.</i> a surfboard

>  In hindsight, the two boards I owned - the first an oversized "goat boat" or "dunger" in surfer-speak - seem secondary to the real, standard surfer equipment: the roof-racks. <i>(Feb 29, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),4</i>)

### goat track
 
 <i>n.</i> a track used by goats, hence a narrow or difficult track

>  Armed offenders squad officers yesterday cleared a section of road known as a goat track - by shooting the goats... Locals call the road a goat track, partly because it is tight and winding in places but also because of the number of goats on or near it. <i>(Dec 11, 2002,Dominion Post</i>)

### goat willow
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [willow](../W#willow))

>  <i>goat willow [s] Salix caprea</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 87 <i>willow, goat [s] Salix caprea</i> L <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,87</i>)

### goat's rue
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> GOAT'S RUE (Galega officinalis ), has been recorded as a weed only in the Manawatu riverbed and adjacent districts as far south as Levin [Wellington]. It is a perennial growing 3 to 4 feet high, with pinnate leaves and lilac-coloured flowers half an inch in length, borne on spikes a foot or more long. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,111,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>goat's rue [s] Galega officinalis</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> GOAT'S RUE (Galega officinalis ) . . . native to Eurasia. it was first recorded from the Manawatu in 1960, and is still essentially confined to the vicinity of their river. . . . <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,63</i>)

### goatfish
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also night walker](new-no.md#night-walker))

>  Bottom-dwelling goatfish use the barbels on their lower jaws to search for food among the sand and shells on the deck. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

>  UPENEUS POROSUS (=UPENEICHTHYS VLAMINGII). <i>Red Mullet</i>. Sold as `goatfish' in Auckland, where it is fairly common. <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,V/93</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Goatfish <i>Barbupeneus signatus</i> (Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED MULLET ... <i>Other names: Upeneichthys porosus</i>; goat-fish; ahuruhuru (Maori <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,238</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> RED MULLET [<i>Upeneichthys porosus</i>] ... Goatfish is another name, as is the Maori ahuruhuru. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GOATFISH <i>See Red Mullet </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 164 MULLET Occur locally in two main types - the Grey and the Yellow-Eye. Other mullet types also occur in our waters but not in any great numbers. While some overlap their names include the Blue-Striped Red Mullet (The Goatfish), the Mangrove Mullet, the Red Mullet plus the River and the Sea Mullets. <i>Ibid.</i> 168 MULLET, RED This is the barbelled Goatfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,115</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GOATFISH Upeneichthys lineatus (Upeneichthys porosus)</i> (Red mullet, ahuruhuru) ... A steeply sloping forehead ends in a small downward facing mouth and beneath the chin are a pair of conspicuous long barbels. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,225,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] red mullet .. [scientific name] <i>Upeneichthys porosus </i>.. [other name(s)] goat fish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] goatfish 1476, 1485 GOATFISHES 1474-86 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Katati </i> - This bird [the banded rail] answers nearest to the godwit, of any I am acquainted with. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),69</i>) [NOTES: [G.J.Griffiths notes: likened the Katatai closest to a godwit, but he did not apply godwit to the Katatai]]

>  A pair of goatfish rest in a darkened corner and show off their night colours of red and purple. <i>(May 24, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,F8</i>)

### gobbler
 
 <i>n.</i> a man who performs fellatio

>  the girls lose their self-confidence [in adolescence], boys become assertive and loud. the [sic] Female becomes an Object of Abuse (Mot, Mole) ... streamers of colourful language, terms of abuse, that delight and appal me (Wanker, Gobbler, Bummer ... [sic] ... i [sic] am convinced by the useage [sic] that they do know the meaning of these words. <i>(1973,Islands,6/385</i>) [NOTES: Body (Diary of a School Music Teacher)]

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> Homosexuality ... is regarded with tolerance by prisoners, fellatio practitioners were derisively known as `gobblers'.<i>Ibid.</i> 97 gobbler a man who commits fellatio <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,70</i>)

### goblin orange
 
 <i>n.</i> a patotara berry

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> [check] ... the vernacular [name] varies in different districts. ... Leucopogon fraseri ... heath, patotara, goblin orange ... Carmichaelia odorata ... broom, wild liquorice ... Muehlenbeckia australis ... grape weed ... Muehlenbeckia complexa ... wirebush <i>(1947,At Omatua's Fireside,127</i>)

### goblin shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [term not specific to NZ; goblin shark is found in NZ waters]

>  The most unusual fish caught was a goblin shark, which usually lives in deeper water. <i>(Jun 6, 2000,Independent Herald (Wellington),6</i>)

>  <i>Mitsukurina owstoni</i>, the goblin shark, is, of course, shy of humans... With all of the six specimens in the bag. the Museum of New Zealand, in Wellington, now has one of the most important collections of goblin sharks in the world. <i>(Jun 18, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### goby
 
 <i>n.</i> various fishes

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Goby <i>Acentrogobius lentiginosus</i> (Richardson) ... Goby <i>Callogobius atratus</i> Griffin ...Goby <i>Favonigobius obliquus</i> (McCulloch and Ogilby) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  [index to common names 283-293] goby [TS] 1990-2001 GOBIES 1990-2002 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> BLACK GOBY <i>Gobiopsis atrata (Callygobius atratus) ... </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,285,1984 ed.</i>)

### God Boy
 
 <i>n.</i> from the title of a novel by Ian Cross

>  As a young teenager he prayed for two things - to be able to stop masturbating and to be a priest. Like the Jimmy Sullivan character in the Ian Cross novel, he was a God Boy. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,66</i>)

### God forbid
 
 <i>n.</i> kid [prisoners' slang]

>  I make a bit of light talk with him; ask about the cheese and god forbids. god forbids....kids <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### God's loan country
 
 <i>n.</i> an early Truth variant of God's own country

>  Christian John Taylor has fallen on evil times in Canterbury. He arrived in God's Loan Country from South Africa Boer War time, and industry being crowded out just then, he tried journalism <i>(Oct 2, 1909,NZ Truth,223/6/2</i>)

>  It [Auckland's nominee Harbour Board] has a complete disregard for public convenience, and its profligate expenditure of loan monies has established a record for recklessness, even in God's loan country. <i>(Feb 19, 1910,NZ Truth,243/1/1</i>)

### God's own
 
 <i>n.</i> ellipsis of God's own country [also citations for full form below] (See [Godzone](go-gra.md#godzone))

>  Like the USA and Australia before it, New Zealand was more than a hundred years ago christened <i>God's Own Country</i>... (<i>DNZE</i> also records the ephemeral variants <i>God's</i> or <i>Ward's Loan Country</i> from the freely borrowing period of Seddon's immediate successor... Shortening quickly produced <i>God's Own</i>, respelt in the 1960s as <i>Godzone</i> (</i>Gordzone, God-Zone</i>, etc.), neatly reintroducing a term of place into the name. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 10</i>)

>  (Howden) ... we are still approaching the last cape ..., our last glimpse of God's own. <i>(Jan 20, 1917,Great Adventure,163,1988 ed.</i>)

>  `Riggs, Sealer and Scoundrel' Ted Wildey [?heading] In the bad old days, this [far south] portion of `God's own' had a reputation, sinister and sanguinary. The Maoris were exceedingly fierce, with an abnormal taste for `long pig' (human flesh). <i>(Sep 15, 1926,Aussie (NZ Section),xvi</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Indeed no, in Enzed God's Own or whatever one would have to wait another ten years. <i>(1977,Never Enough!,97</i>)

>  "Monty" was then despatched to Sydney to help him [sc. the Australian squatter] and his wealthy brothers to information regarding "God's Own." <i>(Jul 12, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> They liked the comfortable official statistics, the patriotic platitudes, the inevitable reference to "God's own country". <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,303</i>) [NOTES: 1924 edition]

>  New Zealand has often claimed on its own behalf that it is "God's own country." <i>(Feb 19, 1915,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  He had read in the papers over there that New Zealand was God's own country. <i>(Oct 6, 1911,Maoriland Worker,16</i>)

>  This Dominion may take unto itself the distinguished title of "God's Own Country," but there are some queer places and conditions to be found in it. <i>(Aug 27, 1919,Alexandra Herald,4</i>)

### God's own country
 
 <i>n.</i> A political or journalistic catchphrase popularised from about the times of Richard Seddon and now used mainly ironically or jocularly: often abbrev. to God's own, Godzone, Gordzone q.v. (See [also Ward's own country](../A#also-ward's-own-country))

>  The earliest recorded use of the phrase was as the title of a poem about New Zealand written by Thomas Bracken sometime in the 1880s. It was published in a book of his poems in 1890, and then again in 1893 in a book containing a selection of his works, entitled Lays and Lyrics: God's Own Country and Other Poems. God's Own Country as a phrase was often used and popularised by New Zealand's longest serving prime minister, Richard John Seddon. He last used it on June 10, 1906 when he sent a telegram to the Victorian premier, Thomas Bent, the day before leaving Sydney to return home to New Zealand. "Just leaving for God's own country," he wrote. He never made it, dying the next day on the ship Oswestry Grange. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God%27s_Own_Country This page was last modified 16:39, 22 December 2005. accessed 27 Jan 2006.]

>  Give me, give me God's own country (from a spieler's point of view),/ Where the scripper and the sharper conjugate the verb `to do'. <i>(Jun 15, 1892,Auckland  Star</i>) [NOTES: in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 82]

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> [A Maorilander in Australia] I'm doing very well [here: check] but I would much sooner live on a far smaller salary in `God's Own [check capO] Country'. <i>(1893,Lays and Lyrics,5</i>)

>  A cool `thou.' is not to be looked askance at at any time, and taxing Chows as they enter Gordzone Country is a profitable bit of business... <i>(Apr 13, 1907,NZ Truth,95/4/4</i>)

>  Maorilanders boom their country ... they brag of its being `God's Own Country'. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/2</i>) [NOTES: 1905-6 Frequent in Truth eg 1905 1/2, 25,26; 1906 35/5/3-4 etc]

>  The Standard Oil Octopus has played the game so often that it really won't hesitate in this case to preserve its monopoly, even in God's Own Country. <i>(Jul 28, 1906,NZ Truth,58/5/8</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> I feel scared sometimes when I hear people talking, but it is God's own country & God wants his own men in & that's what you are. Goodbye - you dear, dear man. [Letter to Fowlds from May Miller] <i>(1980,Fowlds Papers, The</i>) [NOTES: 1908 Oct 27 in 1980 Rogers The Fowlds Papers - Politics 11]

>  <b>POWER</b> So lovely it is - lovable and grand - that the greatest Poet of the Southern Seas [Bracken] did not hesitate to sing of it as God's own country. <i>(1908,New Zealand: The Islands of the Blest,5</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> ... have come back convinced there is no place like `God's own country'. Isn't this war horrible? <i>(1980,Fowlds Papers, The,37</i>) [NOTES: Letter from Fowlds to R. McNab 1914 Oct 8 in 1980 Rogers The Fowlds Papers - Politics 37 1914, 8 Oct -]

>  In the bad old days, this portion of `God's own' had a reputation, sinister and sanguinary. <i>(Sep 15, 1926,Aussie (NZ Section),xvi</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Richard John Seddon ... the popularly acclaimed uncrowned king of the land he himself had named `God's own Country'. <i>(1926,Lenore Divine,20</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Some of the names given to it [sc New Zealand] are very suggestive and often throw light upon its position, character and destiny. It has been called the Britain of the South; the Wonderland of the Pacific; and God's Own Country. Maoriland is another name by which it is known and indicates the native race ... <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,13</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> This is the only complaint that has ever been made by travellers landing in God's own country. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,26</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Richard John Seddon, New Zealand Premier, called New Zealand `God's Own Country' and told of the plenty there could be for all. But we were always poor. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,24,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> ... their own country, which is an annex of the Neo-christian deity himself (God's Own Country, they call it, like `The Queen's own Rifles') ... <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,172</i>)

>  <b>WALL</b> Who, I wonder, filched away/ This blatant name from USA, God's Own Country. <i>(1948,Pioneers and Other Poems, The,188</i>)

>  [Corresp. col] Sir, - There has been some speculation in the columns of `The Post' as to who originated the phrase `God's own country.' If Mr. Seddon did use it first in New Zealand, then he was not being original, since the description had been applied by innumerable politicians in the last century to the United States of America. Ask any American which is God's own country, and he will not hesitate with his answer. It is regrettable that New Zealanders have been foolish enough to copy the phrase.-I am, etc., PEGASUS <i>(Jul 5, 1956,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> They were very soon to be assured that they inhabited `God's Own Country'. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,5</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> If the colony was not yet `God's Own Country', as Seddon alleged, it had secured the reasonable comfort which most of mankind seek. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,189</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Working Man's Paradise we call this country. And everyone believes it. Just like the Pioneers Were Wonderful. God's Own Country. There Are Now Slums. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,21</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> I talked to as many [New Zealanders] as I could and I learnt on [sic] all hands that I was going to God's Own Country. <i>(1959,No Remittance,23</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> But they call me Fritzie! Next they call me Dutchie or Polie or Homie, they will say I haf no right to be in - in - Himmel, which God's Own Country am I in? <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,99</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> [I] may some day have to give Matilda another ride through this spot they call God's own country. Think its [sic] Dick Seddon's mostly. Gets a big hearing with the Maorilanders. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,8</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> `People come out here and cry, `God's own country!' and we stretch our ears in delight, imagining they have cried, `And God's own people too!' <i>(1935,Little Country, The,168,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Thereafter we ... trudged through our first year in `God's own country' like blind men wading through a bog. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,27</i>)

>  Boys, you must remember, right from the jump, that we are nothing more nor less than walking advertisements for New Zealand, "God's own country." <i>(Sep 29, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,55</i>)

### godwit
 
 <i>n.</i> an expatriate NZer

>  For half an hour, Paul Holmes swung his rhetorical cudgels on behalf of New Zealand's reluctant godwits... The mums and dads of the godwits (or as Tony Simpson prefers to call them, the "halfwits") can shake their heads sadly over their gin and tonics and intone indignantly upon the destructive effects "this government's policies" are having on the future of the nation. <i>(Oct 11, 2000,Independent Business Weekly,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A bird of the tidal estuaries which migrates to nest in the Arctic Circle. Limosa sp. kuaka ?katati [check]: ?often erron. called curlew, snipe.

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> Where Bar-tailed Godwits occur in large flocks, other waders will be found. Ohiwa and Tauranga are good `godwit harbours' in the Bay of Plenty, and so are Raglan and Aotea west of the Waikato. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,111</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The end of the lake abounds with wild duck I also saw a fine Bittern a small white Gull a wader like a Godwit and shot a Pukako. <i>(May 15, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,311,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Here ... the Dusky Plover ... and the Southern Godwit (<i>Limosa australis</i>) were in large flocks. The natives call ... the latter, Kuaka. The Godwit is, when in season, very fat, and good eating. [1959 ed. fn.] Pacific Godwit (<i>Limosa baueri</i>) <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion Nthn Isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 44]

>  Limosa Novae Zelandiae. Kuaka. Godwit. East Coast. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: Hutton Birds Great Barrier]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 76. LIMOSA UROPYGIALIS. Gould. Goodwit.[sic] Curlew. Kuaka. L. <i>Novae Zealandiae</i>. GRAY. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,28</i>)

>  LIMOSA BAUERI, <i>Naum.</i> - Godwit. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/126</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> ... what are called curlew here are really godwit ... <i>Ibid.</i> 135 [Chapter heading] CHAPTER XVIII GODWIT SHOOTING <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,31</i>)

>  About the end of November flocks of a ... wading bird appear on the swamp - the godwit, or curlew as it is more commonly called. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/217</i>)

>  [Animals Protection Act] There is a distinction in the case of the godwit (Maori kuaka, or hakakao), the season for which comprises the months of February, March, and April. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,139</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... Mr. Pitcairn ... was killed on Uretara Island, where he was camped shooting<i> kuaka</i> (godwit). <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/314,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <i>ku[u]aka</i>, a migratory bird (Limosa novae zealandiae), godwit; nests in Siberia. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GODWIT (Limosa lapponica), or Kuaka of the Maoris is our best known migrant. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,81</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Out ... at the end of the [Farewell] spit we made acquaintance with the myriads of sea birds that abound there - godwits, or curlews, as they are sometimes called. <i>(1951,Confessions,146</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Limosa novae zealandiae</i> ... Godwit. KUAKA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,81</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Present local authority plans ... could destroy the breeding grounds for ... the rarer bittern, godwit, white-faced heron, shag, pukeko, crake, knot, golden plover and greenshank. <i>(1985,G'day Country,77</i>)

>  [He] suggested the Society should reconsider the decision to fix the opening of the godwit season on February 1. <i>(Jun 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  [He] said that the godwit was a sea-bird, and could never be shot out. <i>(Jun 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  Reports promising a good supply of godwits for the shooting season.. have been received from the Far North. <i>(Feb 1, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> EASTERN (PACIFIC) BAR-TAILED GODWIT <i>Local names</i>: Godwit, Kuaka. (Also, quite incorrectly, `Snipe' and `Curlew'.) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,133</i>)

>  <i>Eastern Bar-tailed Godwit Limosa lapponica baueri</i> NAUMANN, 1836 Bar-tailed godwits start to arrive in New Zealand in conspicuous numbers in the second half of September. ... [195] OTHER NAMES: <i>Kuaka, Pacific godwit. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,194</i>)

### Godzone
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) New Zealand [also l.c. godzone]

>  <b>HULME</b> This godzone baby talk. Hottie lolly cardie nappy, crappy the lot of it, she snarls to herself. But what to say that the kid'd recognise? <i>(1983,Bone People, The,31</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> ...when it sure did reach the Godzone press ... The director general ...had no trouble in keeping MGM out of Godzone [fn] godzone: abbreviation of God's Own Country <i>(1979,I Passed This Way,356,1980 ed.</i>)

>  (R.M.Daniell) Godzone English [heading] Sir, - Out of the `Godzone English' words on the Listener cover, all but two (kainga and kanuka) were already in the Hamlyn World Dictionary, published 1971. <i>(Apr 7, 1979,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Godzone Skies is an excellent introduction to astronomy. <i>(Oct 17, 1992,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Godzone Skies is the first potted history of New Zealand astronomy. <i>(Oct 27, 1992,Dominion,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) New Zealand. Usu. cap. Humorous or ironic shortening and respelling of God's own country q.v.

>  There are two more parties - a party called the Alliance, campaigning vociferously for a socialist Utopia; and "Godzown First" party, campaigning for "Godzown for Godzowners, not for Godzillas". <i>(Oct 12, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Honestly, I don't really care if Kevin Roberts thinks that we should be marketing Godzone with daft images of roast dinners and Formica table tops, or renaming the islands Maui and Pounamu to jazz them up a bit (although I'm not so sure I want my tax dollars spent on that). <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  Marketeers can no longer base their work on old, comfortable perceptions of "Godzone". <i>(Sep 8, 1999,Independent Business Weekly,26</i>)

>  Despite their smugness, too, about the felicities of `God's Own Country' (`Godzone' simply, to the irreverent) ... New Zealanders are not completely lacking in ... self-criticism. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),25</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> In God-Zone you approach different topics with an adjustment of your volume control. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,63</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Godzone: This is Godzone country and don't let nobody tell you different (God's own). The foregoing being understood Godzone is often used as a synonym for NEW ZEALAND `How long have you been in Godzone?' <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,47</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> 2 Or perhaps it would be better still to invite Karl Marx back to Godzone. <i>(1981,Straight from the Shoulder,21</i>)

>  <b>IVORY</b> Godzone God's own Country (New Zealand) <i>(1982,Pacific Index of Abbreviations,136</i>)

>  It could well have been said - again - last week that Sir Robert cut across the prevailing mood of Kiwidom. If God-zone was on the move, he certainly was not, if he could help it. <i>(Jul 30, 1984,National Business Review,15</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `Hell's bells and buggy wheels, a real pommy princess ... How long have you been in godzone.' `Godzone. Is that a Maori expression?' `Godzone country ... is our proud name for ... New bloody Zealand.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,45</i>)

>  godzone n. NZ sl. New Zealand. [God's own (country)] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,323</i>) [NOTES: godzone n. NZ sl. New Zealand. [God's own (country)]

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> `In Godzone. Back home. How were they?' <i>(1986,O.E.,21</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Godzone God's Own Country; also applied to Australia. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)

>  Just as the flavours and textures of non-European foods have become mainstream, so, too, will the arts, culture, language and dress of migrants add colour and life to "Godzone". <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  But because of our smallness, I have a view that we truly are, and I believe it more and more, that New Zealand is Godzone. <i>(Oct 6, 2005,NZ Herald,EG 5</i>)

>  We need to bear in mind that [Bracken] was writing both poetry and a prayer in his anthem, and might have sought a symbol that included the concept of a country blessed by God. Or, as we say today, 'Godzone.' <i>(Aug 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Here I am back in good old New Zealand, Godzone and my home. <i>(Jul 16, 2002,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

>  <b>Allen Curnow</b> As a Decent joker, I don't suggest | That Godzone Country isn't the Best <i>(2005,Whim Wham's New Zealand</i>) [NOTES: (poem originally published 1954)]

>  The new instrumentation allows Geonet to pinpoint quakes and measure the ways in which Godzone is deforming. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  [Hdg] Saying no to family violence in Godzone... [She] is stepping up to tell the 10,000 children taken into refuge each year with their mothers that violence is not meant to be a normal part of growing up in Godzone. <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Ah, Godzone - she was a lovely little country .. Alas, farewell dear Godzone, we knew you well. That's right. No more Godzone, people. This is the new New Zealand - a cold-hearted place where prisoners are about to be forced to give up smoking, where we're now paying for polluting our atmosphere and where MPs are the only ones who look even remotely likely to get a pay rise. <i>(Jul 3, 2010,Daily Post (Rotorua),A6</i>)

>  In Godzone our cattle are grass-fed and roam free, with no antibiotics. <i>(Mar 26, 2012,NZ Farmers' Weekly,30</i>)

>  I don't believe that referring to New Zealand as Godzone is just a colloquialism. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Wilderness,11</i>)

### GOFD test
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a 'germination only field dressed' test that indicates healthy germination of field-dressed seed.

>  .. seed producers are encouraged to use the free GOFD test (GOFD stands for germination only field dressed) to indicate the germination of their field-dressed seed. Results of this test will show if germination damage has occurred .. For a GOFD test several subsamples should be drawn from throughout the harvested seed lot.. <i>(Sep 1973,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)
