### going off, man
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  A project on slang saw the Lytton third formers explain that "chur bro" was a greeting and "going off man" meant really neat, and the Welsh students said "munting" meant ugly, "Mosher" was someone who liked grunge music, and "skank" meant not fair. <i>(Jul 3, 1997,NZ Infotech Weekly,21</i>)

### going round the horn
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a drinking game (cf. Hokitika swindle)

>  `We were doing what is generally called `going round the horn' for drinks'. Judge `That is a game of chance, of course; by which it was decided who should pay for refreshments. <i>(1866,Maungatapu Murders,51</i>)

### gold
 
 <i>adj.</i> goldmining

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> At length the gold days and Cobb's coaches ended this uncertain travel, and now that ... journey is accomplished in an hour-and-a-half. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,108</i>)

### gold box
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  Cheyne then got a board and one of the plushes from the manager's office, laid the plush on the board, and fixed the board from the gold box to the streaming down pipe. Cheyne then took a quantity of sand out of the box, put it on the board, and turned the water on, and washed the sand back into the gold box. The gold remained on the plush. <i>(Feb 25, 1905,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Feb 25 Otago Daily Times in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 42]

>  He shovels the black sand into the gold box, washes it through with water pumped from the lagoon on the other side of the sand hills. The gold falls on to the plush (`I just use an ordinary towel') which is then shaken into a baby's bath. <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)

### gold broker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> There can be no doubt that for good clean gold, ready market at fair prices will be found. ALBERT WM. HANSARD,/ Gold Broker. <i>(1853,Auckland,109</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 21 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 109]

### gold buyer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The gold buyer from the Bank of New Zealand ... rushed out to see the fun, ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,43</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> They mistook George Dobson for a gold-buyer named Fox, who they heard was going to Greymouth with the gold he had bought, ... <i>(1930,Reminiscences,123</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> On one occasion he described how two lucky miners collected two billies full of gold ...; how the gold buyer on his rounds gave them a check [sic] for [pounds]2,000 ... <i>(1931,Such Is Life,50</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> He is carrier, ...gold buyer, slaughterman, ... land agent, station-holder, ... etc. etc. <i>(1971,Wakatip,83</i>)

### Gold Circular
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> A few days after the sale [11 December 1852], the following Gold Circular was published, describing the quantity, character, and other particulars of the parcels of gold which had recently arrived from the [Thames] goldfield. <i>(1853,Auckland,105</i>)

### Gold Coast
 
 <i>n.</i> the Wellington suburban west coast.

>  <b>KNOX</b> We may have taken Mike's car up the Gold Coast at Anniversary Weekend, and cooled our wine in the rockpools of Pukerua Bay ... <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,208</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> The Manawatu and the Gold Coast are very much the home leg for me, a route I could drive ... in my sleep. <i>(1985,G'day Country,74</i>)

>  Bad news for those of us of simple pleasures. Two Gold Coast residents have written to me complaining about this new scheme of dredging for pipis in-shore along the Paraparaumu beaches. <i>(Jul 11, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the South Island West Coast

>  <b>DILKE</b> Even sailors on shore `for a run' here wear cord breeches and high tight-fitting boots, often armed with spurs, though, as there are no horses except those of the Gold-Coast Police, they cannot enjoy much riding. <i>(1868,Greater Britain,1/335</i>)

### gold coin
 
 <i>n.</i> a common generic name for $1 and $2 coins. [Jim Cameron writes : "Admission by gold coin" often used in relation to minor events,eg. notice in <i>Wairarapa News</i> 15/9/1999]

>  All parishioners and friends are very welcome - gold coin (or more!) donation to assist with the CWL work in the Missions. <i>(Oct 13, 2002,Onslow Parish Newsletter,2</i>)

### Gold Commissioner
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Commissioner](chi-con.md#commissioner))

### gold diggings
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka gold-diggings]

>  I found Mr. Heaphy actively employed in superintending the Gold Diggings [on the Kapanga River] ... <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/182</i>) [NOTES: Ligar's Report]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [He] has just abandoned his wife and set off for the gold diggings. <i>(Mar 4, 1852,Journal,7/278</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Considering the small number of diggers yet upon the ground - not more than forty persons, ... - the short time since the first specks of gold were discovered - ... and the large proportion of the time of each digger employed in exploring , we hail this commencement of the Auckland gold diggings as affording the most brilliant promise of ultimate productiveness. <i>(1853,Auckland,107</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 13 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 107]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> `gold diggins' [several times passim in quotation marks] <i>Ibid.</i> 56 Although at ordinary times there is a fair supply, the newly discovered `gold diggins' had at [57] the period ... greatly increased ... the value of horses in Australia. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,51</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The gold diggings have produced their effect upon this colony. <i>(Jan 30, 1853,Traveller under Concern,73,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> We only got two passengers here [at Nelson] for the [Australian] gold-diggings. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,46</i>)

>  ...not withstanding the great rise in price of stock in Australia, ... owing to the continued excitment of the gold diggings, a moderate number of sheep ... were imported ... <i>(1854,Canterbury Almanac, The,59</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> In fact, except among the crowds at the gold diggings ... I have never heard of a person in absolute want ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),290</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... we came to a broader road, which led to the gold-diggings situated higher up; ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,85</i>)

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> [Colonial slang] If a man isn't `up to the mark' he is called `a regular shicer!' This phrase originated from the gold diggings. Supposing a man is digging, but without success, and he therefore abandons his hole' it is called a `shicer' - that is, the ground was called a `shicer' or duffer, thus the origin. <i>(1887,Taken In,135</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> This Harry, still young in years, was one of those men who, having spent most of their adult existence at the gold-diggings in one or other of the Colonies, ... <i>(1889,In the Shadow of the Bush,13</i>)

### gold drift
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf drift](dou-dyn.md#drift))

>  `Gold drift' consists of sand and gravel containing gold, and is formed by the action of running water in streams, or water in motion, as along a sea or lake beach. <i>(1869,TrNZI,371</i>) [NOTES: Hector]

### gold escort
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also escort](ens-eye.md#escort))

>  It is very impressive to see the close contact of violence and order indicated by the armed gold escort amidst the busy street of Sydney: a long phaeton with four seats one behind another, with moustachioed men in plain dresses intended for work, with glistening muskets and pistols, and perhaps a lady passenger in the middle, it is I suppose the gold carriage, and a van and rear-guard of horsemen attend this. <i>(Mar 31, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/146,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Richmond)]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> ... the first gold escort left Tuapeka for Dunedin on 18th July, <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,5</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Southern Provinces Almanac in 1868 Lindsay Contributions to NZ Botany 5]

>  There are a great number of people at the station this evening. Mr Sherman the head of the Police and Gold escort. All police here are mounted and have pistols swords etc. <i>(Jul 18, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,194,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The first gold escort, in August, brought down 5056 ounces. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,22</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> The gold escort was expected, and they agreed to murder all who came that road that day. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,78</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> In the latter end of the 'sixties, the gold escort from Waimea to Hokitika consisted of a troop of mounted policemen. <i>Ibid.</i> 134 I once went to Charleston ... in company with the Gold Escort, and it was a picture to see those fine troopers turn out, `armed to the teeth', as the saying is. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,63</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Among other duties they formed the gold escort, and once a fortnight brought the gold to town [Dunedin]. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,15</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> The Government gold escort was there [at the site of the coach break-down], and advised Miles not to proceed, <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,35</i>)

>  <b>ODELL</b> It was so named because police barracks were erected there at the time of the gold escort fiasco. ... The gold escort travelled only once, and then with about one ounce of gold. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,52</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> ... a bank officer having been stuck up and robbed of some gold, the Provincial Council resolved to institute an armed gold escort. <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,265</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The gold escort stopped at Mr Adam's accommodation house, and I remember seeing one lot that stopped for dinner. The gold was taken off the pack horses and one guarded it while the others had dinner. <i>Ibid.</i> 18 The gold escort from Switzers to Invercargill also passed through Gore for a long time after this date, and usually consisted of a banker with the gold and two armed troopers or mounted police. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,15,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> Among them was the gold escort, and one of the policemen put his foot through the bottom of the whaleboat trying to get out the heavy box of gold. <i>(1971,Wakatip,88</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> It was thought best to appoint Jackson Keddell, who was already in charge of the Gold Escort, as Commissioner, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 19 The first Gold Escort left Clyde for Dunedin on 3 October 1862, with 6031 oz. gold securely strapped on packhorses, under the supervision of Major Bracken and three troopers. 1861, ... <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,13</i>)

>  .. I may state that the various amounts of gold sent down to Dunedin by escort ... Of the first two escorts there is no official record at hand, but they may be roughly estimated at a total of 3,500oz. ... The next escort is expected to arrive this evening. <i>(1861,NZ Goldfields,40,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Oct 4 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 40.]

### gold fever
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The symptoms of the `Gold Fever' begin to be daily more distinct. <i>(Sep 2, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  Fine. The gold fever has taken every other man in the place, ... <i>(Dec 1, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,56,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... numbers of men have left their ordinary occupations, smitten with the general gold fever, ... <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The excitement soon became intense. Thousands were bitten by the gold fever, and abandoned their ordinary pursuits to `try their luck' at the diggings. ... <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago,51,1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Oct in 1887 Pyke History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago (1962) 51]

>  <b>HAAST</b> The gold said to have been obtained during the gold fever of 1865-70 in the Malvern Hills ... proved to be either iron pyrites ... or to put upon it a charitable construction, ... had been lost by accident. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,465</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I took the gold fever, and in company with another man travelled overland to Melbourne. <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,18</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... for what, after all, is this gold-fever [110] but gambling in its most incurable form. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,109</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Mar 12 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 109-110]

>  <b>COAD</b> The two brothers Hitching had caught the gold-fever, and had forsaken their ordinary avocations ... to try their luck on the newly discovered field. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,48</i>)

>  <b>AYSON THOMAS</b> Thomas went to work at Anderson's at Boundary Creek. By this time there was another epidemic of gold fever. <i>(1937,In May 1863,42</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Their station life was their main concern, and when the gold fever broke out not a one abandoned it for the fierce quest of sudden wealth. <i>(1945,Historical Southland,108</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Mr Smith was not infected with the `gold fever' and took no great part in the Waikaka dredging boom. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,12,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `You've got the gold fever,' said Melody, making up our order. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,50,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> But he had the gold fever really and truly, and though he got no gold he continued to work this claim till he was unable to walk the distance. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,85</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> Throughout the gold fever of the 1860s it remained the basic element in the southern economy. <i>(1963,Story of New Zealand, The,107</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> Thousands were smitten with gold fever and it seemed that every man who could move was obsessed with the one aim: ... <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,12</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> Smitten with gold fever, he set off for diggings some 300 miles from Sydney, then joined the rush to Gabriel's Gully in Otago, but he had no luck. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,23</i>)

### gold fields
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> . [plural use]

>  <b>FITTON</b> There has been nothing doing with the Coromandel Gold Fields near Auckland. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),69</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The gold-fields on this coast were only discovered eight months ago, and already several canvas towns have sprung up; there are thirty thousand diggers at work ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,16,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> ...goldfields. <i>(1868,Letters & Journals,IV/533</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> [He] had tried his luck for a time on the gold-fields of the West Coast, where he had picked up Big George for a mate. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,85</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Whether it were through the bush-fringed Christchurch road or along the straight stretches of road that led from Stafford and the goldfields of Goldsborough to Kumara when old Fanny pulled the family buggy, those drives were a joy ... <i>(1968,Seddons, The,30</i>)

### gold flake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> In the specimens of the gold-bearing veins of quartz of felspartick appearance, the gold flakes stand boldly out without any foreign substance between, and are easily bent or broken off : ... <i>(1853,Auckland,112</i>)

### gold hunter
 
 <i>n.</i>

### gold kiwifruit/golden kiwifruit
 
 <i>n.</i> a hairless golden-skinned and fleshed variety of kiwifruit

>  Plantings of gold kiwifruit will be constrained for a third successive year, boosting the premiums earned by existing growers... <i>(Dec 11, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,C5</i>)

>  This year's crop of golden kiwifruit is estimated to be more than 10 times bigger than last year's. <i>(Feb 26, 2000,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Zespri is facing a big challenge in selling an extra two million trays of the new gold kiwifruit variety on world markets ... <i>(May 18, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  However, 605,000 cases of this has been due to growth in gold kiwifruit sales. New entrants face what could be a big hurdle as the Zespri board has a moratorium on any new plantings of gold fruit... Growing gold is not a pot of gold for the grower unless conditions are right. <i>(Sep 12, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Aside from the colour and the smoother skin, gold kiwifruit have a more tropical flavour and.. unlike the green ones they do not lose their lovely colour when cooked. <i>(Jul 24, 2003,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

>  What do you think of golden kiwifruit? The Zespri marketers describe them as a "sunburst of silken flesh with juices like nectar, wrapped in a smooth bronze skin." <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The local history of golden kiwifruit (<i>Actinidia chinenis</i> is much shorter [sc. than green kiwifruit]. Only in 1977 were seeds brought from Beijing Botanical Gardens to be planted at a research orchard in Te Puke. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The new gold kiwifruit - introduced on international markets for the first time this year - has run into teething problems... Production of gold kiwifruit was up from 300,000 trays last year to five million, and organics increased threefold to three million. <i>(Nov 24, 2000,Dominion,17</i>)

### gold mania
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The gold-mania at Sydney ... <i>(Jul 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

### Gold Receiver
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WOOD</b> This they lodged at Dunedin [in 1862] with the Chief Gold Receiver and, when granted a reward ..., they revealed the location of their find. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,5</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> As it was now very likely that others would soon be arriving on the scene, Hartley and Reilly decided to go to the Gold Receiver in Dunedin with whom they lodged 87 pounds of gold. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,11</i>)

### gold saving table
 
 <i>n.</i> [West Coast goldminers']

>  <b>HEINZ</b> A novel method of elevating in use in Westland was the `Blow up.' The principle is similar to the hydraulic elevator, but in reverse ... The water pressure forced the sands and gravels to the surface onto the gold saving tables. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,48</i>)

>  Mr Johnston (the Chairman) read the following list of probable exhibits .. Brooke-Smith - Gold saving table. <i>(Jun 7, 1889,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  T. JONES, the patentee of the Novelty Gold Saving Tables, is now prepared to erect, and grant licenses to Dredging or Sluicing Companies for the use of his patent, a plan of which can be seen erected on the Grey River Company's dredge. <i>(Sep 26, 1900,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### gold tax
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> For a while, two million pounds worth of gold a year was recovered, and as Canterbury had jurisdiction over the whole area and collected the gold-taxes, the general prosperity may be imagined. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,66</i>)

### gold top
 
 <i>n.</i> beer? (or champagne ?)

>  It may be that Jessie was biassed the other day, but she swore that the bosses [sic] wife was a gay spark, as she sat on customers' knees in the office when they shouted gold top. [a court case on the New Criterion Hotel, Chch.] <i>(Sep 21, 1907,NZ Truth,118/5/2</i>)

### gold watch
 
 <i>n.</i> a good or important person [ca 1930 [? reference] -->ca1987 taxi despatcher `You're a gold watch' to cab-driver who can fulfil a waiting or difficult order.]

### gold workings
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka workings]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The Dunstan gold workings were of two kinds - technically known as `beach claims' and `bank claims'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iv/17,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> In order to visit the gold workings at and near Kanieri township, I crossed the river from Woodstock, ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,111</i>)

>  A small leader has been cut in the company's workings beyond the slide which is showing good gold. <i>(Sep 29, 1879,Thames Star,2</i>)

>  The treatment of the trial parcels of quartz from the various workings in this mine will be finished on Thursday. <i>(Jul 26, 1887,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  "Obtained 26lb picked stone from workings above No. 3 level; gold showing." <i>(Feb 19, 1891,Auckland  Star,8</i>)

>  SOME OF THE OLD WORKINGS IN SHOTOVER GULLY. <i>(Aug 11, 1909,Otago Witness,47</i>)

### gold zone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We ... did not locate payable gold. As a matter of fact we passed the gold zone as it is now recognised, as the alluvial only extends a certain distance back from the coast line <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,42</i>)

### Gold's petrel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are also a few of Gold's petrel [not in Hutton's book] to be found on Cabbage Island among the mutton birds. <i>(Nov 8, 1905,Otago Witness,84</i>)

### gold-bag
 
 <i>n.</i> [See also shammy-bag]

>  <b>Taylor</b> [fn] `He remembered seeing the goldbag at his feet but he was so cold and worn out that he never thought of picking it up.' [Barrington had thrown away his swag and most of its contents in desperate straits.] <i>(1959,Early Travellers,419</i>) [NOTES: 1864 July 20 Barrington reported and quoted in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 419]

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> At the Five-mile diggers carried the gold dust to Okarito to sell in billies, their ordinary chamois leather gold-bags being too small to contain their rich harvests. <i>(1906,Life & Work of Seddon,12</i>)

### gold-bait
 
 <i>n.</i> whitebait

>  Mr Griffiths says whitebait can drive people mad. "It's like gold. That's why they call it gold-bait ..." <i>(Aug 16, 2005,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### gold-bearing
 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>EVANS</b> This dredge received its name from the fact that the McGeorge Brothers prospected for and found a new `deep lead' or layer of gold-bearing wash deeper down and separated from the layer that other dredges in the area had worked upon. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,39</i>)

### gold-belt
 
 <i>n.</i> [universal term - SA, Canada for example. JB]

>  ... had the diggers, instead of going up Rolling river, crossed it at its junction with the Wangapeka river, opposite the saddle, and have gone westward up the Wangapeka to its Source, they would ... have got into the richest portion of the gold-belt. <i>(Mar 23, 1861,Otago Witness,10</i>)

### gold-buying
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> There was only one case of the sticking-up of a banker in Otago, when Skinner ... had been robbed when returning from a gold-buying expedition to the Nevis. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,27</i>)

### gold-coach
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> Diggers still clambered eagerly over the hills but the gold was brought home with dignity. It must have been impressive to see the gold-coach come swinging down the cutting accompanied by a mounted escort, Branigan's men,... <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,36</i>)

### gold-combing
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Once I was nearly going gold-combing with him, but I changed my mind and went to quod instead.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,217</i>)

### gold-country
 
 <i>n.</i>

### gold-crushing
 
 <i>n.</i> [universal, delete]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The gold was found both in quartz fragments and scattered through clay and sand; ... Gold-crushing, it was believed, would prove productive. <i>(1959,Story of New Zealand, The,2/198</i>)

### gold-dig
 
 <i>v.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>AMOS WILBY</b> - Went to Greymouth - got married - went to Nelson Creek - gold digging on Try Again Terrace - <i>(1866,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Diary in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 116 1866]

>  <b>SMALL</b> [I] Bought a pick and a spade, and a-gold-digging went./ <i>(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,27,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I then went to Waimangaroa, where I myself tried gold digging. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,50</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> Within a radius of some three miles about 12,000 men were gold-digging. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,152</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He was gold-digging there. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,80</i>)

### gold-digger
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; aka gold digger. NB: modern usage tends to add gold to differentiate from simplex digger `soldier'.]

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> When the gold-diggers began streaming up the ridge of Maungatua, above the school, they were regarded by the local people as somewhat of a menace. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,36</i>)

>  And a gold-digger's jumper to wear on pay-day,/ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,30</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Sep in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 30]

>  <b>A.S. THOMSON</b> In November [1852] I visited the diggings [at Thames], and procured the frog which is herewith sent. [221] It was got in this way. The gold-diggers were washing the soil of a mountain-stream in the machine called `long-tom'. <i>(1853,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal,55/66-69</i>) [NOTES: 1853 A.S. THOMSON Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal 55/66-69 in 1920 Nov NZJST 3/220-221]

>  <b>J.MATTHEWS</b> Twenty-five ships are waiting there for supplies of this edible for the gold-diggers. <i>(Feb 12, 1854,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Feb 12 J.Matthews Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 192]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> But it was soon observed that many of the gold-diggers came back; <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/176,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> Nature never cut you out for a gold-digger. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,65</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> Why shouldn't he, the first arrival, have the right to take up his pre-emptive option? ... he made the most of it when he appeared before the Waste land Board on February 11 and 13, 1863. `It was about four months ago,' he told the board ..., `that I realised I would be upset by gold-diggers. As a result I have been forced to apply for my pre-emptive rights sooner than I would otherwise have done.' <i>(1971,Wakatip,97</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Feb W.G. Rees in 1971 Griffiths King Wakatip 97]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> A man, to be a good digger, must ... learn how to be one; for gold-digging has become quite a recognised and separate department of labour, and the gold-digger of the present day is ... a comparatively respectable, quiet man ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,229</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Quite 8000 gold-diggers set out from Otago for the new Eldorado. <i>Ibid.</i> 82 Numbers of tents were put up ... for the accommodation of gold-diggers, cattle-drivers, and storekeepers. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,66</i>)

>  They [robins] are encouraged ... by the gold-diggers ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/215</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Westland, or `West Coast', as it is more commonly called, was rushed by gold-diggers in the early sixties, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ the desire to obtain gold by digging. The word is more especially applied to the period between 1851 and 1857, the early Australian discovery of gold. The term had been previously applied in a similar way to the Californian excitement in 1848-49. Called also Yellow-fever. [1888] <i>(1898,Austral English,164</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We left Nelson ... with several gold diggers and a miscellaneous lot of cargo. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,49</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> A number of gold-diggers were working in the neighbourhood, some on the beaches and some on the adjoining terraces. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,141</i>)

### gold-digging
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  Those on the ground were new to gold-digging life, its trials, and its necessities; ... <i>(Oct 4, 1861,NZ Goldfields,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COOPER</b> Thus it will be seen that ... the community have suffered a loss ... in the gold-digging enterprise. <i>(Dec 14, 1867,Digger's Diary,23,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> It was 1874-75 before the old die-hards ... became reconciled to the gold-digging invasion and consented to mining rights over their lands. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,112</i>)

### gold-dish
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>WOOD</b> During prospecting the gold was separated from the dirt by hand panning in a gold-dish, but it was slow, tiring work. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,7</i>)

### gold-dredging
 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The Okarito Gold Dredging Co. was one of the first ... at the beginning of New Zealand's somewhat disastrous gold-dredging boom. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,123</i>)

### gold-getter
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; The term seems to be applied to a person and also to a machine or set up that provides access to good amounts of gold.]

>  . . anad so it was contended that if Government were established the gold-getter would be quite content to sell his winnings to them for, say, GBP4 1s per ounce, and if no expense were entailed in the purchase, the 2s over would give the Government about GBP20,000 per annum . . . <i>(May 31, 1894,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> During his sojourn on the diggings the narrator became acquainted with some gold-getters whose names are writ large on Otago's mining history. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,169</i>)

>  What a steady gold-getter the Perseverance dredge is! <i>(Sep 5, 1900,Otago Witness,34</i>)

### gold-hunting
 
 <i>n.</i> [universal? Shared at least with Aust]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Peters had persuaded a man named Jenkins to accompany him in his gold-hunting expeditions, and for some time Jenkins `fossicked' about the Woolshed, whilst Peters went further ahead <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago,37,1962 ed.</i>)

### gold-mining
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COAD</b> [Hdg] AN INCIDENT OF THE GOLD-MINING DAYS IN NEW ZEALAND <i>(1931,Such Is Life,48</i>)

### gold-prospecting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I take the liberty of troubling you with a short report on the result of a gold-prospecting tour which I commenced about a fortnight since, and which occupied me about ten days. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,4</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Gabriel Read letter in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 4]

### gold-reefing
 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining; also Aus]

>  If only a quarter of what is predicted for this field eventuates the the Paparoa will soon become known far and wide as the richest gold-reefing country in the colony. <i>(1900,NZ Herald</i>) [NOTES: Year is incorrect]

>  <b>COOPER</b> Men are working here with an energy ... which I suppose can only be equalled ... in other gold-reefing districts. <i>(1867,Digger's Diary,8,1978 ed.</i>)

>  The result of the testing of the specimens of quartz from the Wairoa yeserday at the Bank of New Zealand has, perhaps, somewhat damped the hopes of some of our gold-reefing enthusiasts. <i>(Jul 1, 1868,NZ Herald,3</i>)

### gold-rush
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> The dancing rooms, prostitutes, billiard rooms, grog-shops ... were among the inevitable facilities in gold-rush Queenstown. <i>(1971,Wakatip,112</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Ghost-towns with their shells of gold-rush pubs have not yet been smothered by bush or cleared to make roads. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,108</i>)

### gold-saving
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>MAY</b> DEAD WORK: all preparatory work on a claim - sinking shafts, driving tunnels, constructing a race - before gold-saving commences. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

### gold-seeker
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; Cf Goldseekers and Bushrangers Elder. Note Aust and Canadian use of this term]

>  <b>WEKEY</b> The consequence [of many leaving ill-equipped] was, that some of those who carried up provisions with them were `stuck up' on the road, and the principles of communism were practically realised on the way to the Dunstan El Dorado. The first detachment of gold-seekers gradually arrived ... <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,51</i>)

>  ... this non-discovery of payable auriferous ground was in great part due to the inexperience [159] and lack of knowledge of the gold-seekers. <i>(1931,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/158-159</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> If they had known of the hard work put in two years previously ... thus enabling the goldseekers to prospect it uninterruptedly, the two lucky prospectors ... should have ... thanked their ... benefactors. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,145</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> In the bush near Jacksons on the banks of the Teremakau River not far from Kumara there are mounds which mark the place where three gold seekers died. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,37</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> The Rees family barely had time to get settled again at their home station before the quiet pastoral barony ... was invaded by a noisy and often drunken avalanche of gold-seekers. <i>(1971,Wakatip,73</i>)

### gold-wash
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  Gold-wash was got at the edge of a creek. Thirty men are now on the ground. No work is however done yet in any of the claims, but the prospectors say the value of the dicovery cannot be stated. <i>(Jan 9, 1884,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  . . for letters patent . . Francis William Payne, Dunedin, an improved apparatus for separating magnetic sand from gold-wash. . . <i>(Dec 23, 1899,Otago Daily Times,12</i>)

>  We also find that the richest gold-wash always occurs on the inner or concave side of the curves in the course of a river. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.5 (NS): 38</i>) [NOTES: (Park: Cromell)]

### gold-washer
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> ... they despatched the late Mr. William Crawley ... with a load of timber prepared for miners' cradles for gold-washing. <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,30</i>)

### golden  oyster
 
 <i>n.</i>

Commonly found around Auckland and north of Auckland <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,19</i>)

>  The shell I have in my hand is a bivalve commonly known as a golden oyster though it is not a true oyster. ... they usually come from Auckland or further north. <i>(1988,Short Stories from NZ,70</i>)

### golden akeake
 
 <i>n.</i> Olearia paniculata, ?semi-cultivated as an ornamental and hedge plant.

>  Olearia forsteri golden akeake <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### golden bell
 
 <i>n.</i>

### golden chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  golden chain [=] laburnum [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29</i>)

### Golden Coast
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the Wellington west coast from Paramata north to (say) Paraparaumu (See [also Gold Coast](go-gra.md#gold-coast))

>  The Golden Coast has been dulled by leaden skies and as I write is in a sodden state. Month after month of what the Wellington watersiders call `wharfies' sunshine' have checked its growth. ... At the end of the war the Golden Coast was scattered with baches where Wellington people spent summer weekends. <i>(Oct 1963,Comment,17/11</i>)

>  <b>REILLY</b> Sometimes on the weekends I drive out to the so-called Golden Coast - as far away as Peka Peka Beach sometimes ... <i>(1986,Deputy Head,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> West Coast (South Island.) (From the rich gold deposits once there.) (See [also Gold Coast](go-gra.md#gold-coast))

>  <b>REID</b> This was the first recognition of `the Golden Coast'. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,122</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The diggers' story, or, Tales and reminiscences of the Golden Coast, from Westland's earliest pioneers. By Carl J. Pfaff. Issued by the West Coasters' Association to commemorate the Westland Goldfields Jubilee and the unveiling of the pioneers' memorial. [Wellington 1914] [title] <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The</i>)

### Golden Coaster
 
 <i>n.</i> one who lives on Wellington's Golden Coast.

>  Golden Coasters are not, by and large, keen gardeners. Perhaps the heavy clay deters them. More likely the Coast attracts a different type: the open air fraternity which prefers boating and fishing ... The fishing off Mana and Kapiti Islands is good. <i>(Oct 1963,Comment,17/11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> The New Zealand Australian Rules Team [From Tim Lovell-Smith (Tim.Lovell.Smith@natlib.govt.nz)]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a national contest for excellence in wool fleeces.

### golden jandal
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Golden Kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a national lottery in the period 1962-89 (replaced by Instant Kiwi)

>  A third of people never bet at all, a third has the occasional fling, maybe on a Melbourne Cup sweep or a Golden Kiwi ticket, and the remainder bets more than five times a year. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  If any extra funds can be raised from industry, Golden Kiwi, etc. then more colour can be added. <i>(Nov 1987,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,78/21</i>)

>  The New Zealand Lotteries Commission - reponsible for Lotto and the Golden Kiwi - estimated early this year that nearly 60 per cent of the population could be involved in a Lotto jackpot draw. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  'When you win the Golden Kiwi you can buy yourself a big house with a dozen bedrooms,' she'd say. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,92</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> [Hdg] The end of the Golden Kiwi [sc. Keith Holyoake]. <i>(1981,Snakes & leaders,60</i>)

>  For Mother's Day next year a Golden Kiwi ad will feature a Maori mother, breakfasting in bed and being surprised with her family's gift of a lottery ticket.... [24]A recent Golden Kiwi ad purports to represent New Zealand, but not a single non-white face appears. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> Gambling was officially sanctified in 1961 when the old Art Union was renamed the Golden Kiwi. <i>(1991,Kiwi,59</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> He'd spent his day like many other Pumaharians - reading the morning newspaper,.. searching through the Golden Kiwi results; or on Tuesdays waiting for the Bonus Bond results. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,67</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> And if you immediately picture something like the elderly gentlemen who operate their Golden Kiwi/Moa/Takahe booths from central city shop doorways, then picture again. <i>(1985,Moaville Magic,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (in Maori contexts) = golden handshake

>  The $195,000 payout John Tamihere received from the Waipareira Trust was "golden koha", Act leader Rodney Hyde said yesterday... Mr Hyde said Labout could call Mr Tamihere's "golden koha" an elephant for all he cared. <i>(Oct 25, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  The Labour party can call John's "golden koha" an elephant for all I care. <i>(Oct 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### golden lily
 
 <i>n.</i>

### golden line
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining obs.]

Stretching as it does for a distance of over eighty miles, it was called the Golden Line and all the well known mining districts ... were contained in it. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,38</i>)

### Golden Mile
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to a main city shopping street; in Wellington, Lambton Quay

>  The various shopping malls [of Wellington] are perfectly suited to shopping and dining for the family and areas like the Golden Mile (Lambton Quay) suit the business man on a lunch break. <i>(Nov 12, 2001,Northern Courier,9</i>)

### Golden Oldie
 
 <i>n.</i> [1st Golden Oldies social rugbytournament organised in NZ in 1979 by Tom Johnson and Brian Craies on an idea of Craies for rugby players who must be over 35]

>  While at Silver Fern, Ken was one of the pioneers of the "Golden Oldies" sporting festivals concept. <i>(Apr 16, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> We took the Second World War as the starting point [for rugby greats] ... - many of the `golden oldies' have had their stories told often, ... and as their careers are beyond my own I would only have been rewriting what others had said. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,8</i>)

>  Christchurch Mayor Vicki Buck said.. that while normal men thought about sex 30 percent of the time, Golden Oldies thought about it 83 percent. <i>(Oct 6, 1995,Evening Post,29</i>)

>  The Oldies has become identified throughout the world as a Kiwi inspiration; and not a little glory has accrued from this and the insistence that.. all newcomers adhere to the festival's motto of 'Fun, Friendship and Fraternity'. <i>(Oct 6, 1995,Evening Post,29</i>)

### golden oldie
 
 <i>n.</i> an elderly person

>  <b>MANTELL</b> The three golden oldies looked up expectantly when he approached, nodded sagely when he told them police would be along shortly to take charge. <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,21</i>)

>  [Hdg] Charity gathering of golden oldies. <i>(Sep 7, 2000,Evening Post,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [silver pine, <i>Manoao colensoi</i>, formerly <i>Dacrydium colensoi</i> the timber of which goes golden with age]

### Golden Pliers
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a national contest for the construction of farm fences.

>  Beers, who, nine times a winner of fencing's ultimate prize, the Golden Pliers, is the Michael Schumacher of the fencing world. <i>(Jun 16, 2005,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

>  Again, Taranaki had a presence with six-time New Zealand fencing champion Paul van Beers, going for the Golden Pliers. <i>(Jun 16, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

### golden plover
 
 <i>n.</i> Pluvialis fulva

>  Thus the cry of the golden plover heard by night is listened to with dread. <i>(May 16, 1891,Bush Advocate,7</i>)

>  There were 22 head of game, consisting of larks, snipes, woodcocks, teal, French and English partridges, grouse, widgeons, wild ducks, blackcocks, pheasants, a leveret, a hare, and golden plovers. <i>(Jul 14, 1847,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 62. CHARADRIUS FULVUS. Gml. Golden Plover. <i>C. xanthocheilus</i>. GOULD. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,24</i>)

### Golden Queen
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of peach

>  Fish and chips can be eaten from the newspaper with the tomato sauce, made by Mum in the huge preserving pan at the end of each summer, and put back in the bottles that the vinegar was in, after she's done the cases of golden queens which are sent on the train from Hawke's Bay. <i>(Dec 31, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

### golden rod
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Verbascum thapsus</i> flannel leaf (See [mullein](mob-mys.md#mullein))

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLANNEL LEAF (Verbascum thapsus ) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds . . . in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967 ed.</i>)

>  VERBASCUM THAPSUS, a weed of poor land has many popular names. In New Zealand it is called native tobacco, velvet, flannel leaf and golden rod. <i>(Nov 22, 1974,Dominion,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> moth mullein

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MOTH MULLEIN (Verbascum [virgatum ]), also called golden rod, is [not] Cheeseman says `not uncommon in pastures and waste places in both islands'. It is biennial, slender, up to 4 ft. in height, . . . <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,157,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>golden-rod [=] moth mullein [s] </i>...<i> golden-rod [s] Solidago </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29</i>)

### Golden Shears
 
 <i>n.</i> the New Zealand national shearing contest (1961-); also the winner's award.

>  Besides his domination of the Golden Shears, the prestige event on the shearing calendar, Fagan has won 11 national titles and four world crowns, the first in 1988... Fagan spread his 13 Golden Shears titles over 16 years. <i>(Mar 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  The 2002 Golden Shears sheep shearing and wool handling compeition promises to deliver the same action packed final as ever with World Champion David Fagan back in town and well in form. <i>(Jan 23, 2002,Capital Times,10</i>)

>  A Golden Shears contest in the United Kingdom is to be staged in liaison with the Golden Shears International Shearing Championship Society in Masterton. <i>(Jan 4, 1964,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The army had to be called in to control the crowd when the inaugural Golden Shears was staged in 1961. <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Capital Times,16</i>)

>  World-Class shearing champion David Fagan won his 13th Golden Shears championship title in Masterton on Saturday night. <i>(Mar 5, 2001,Dominion,1</i>)

>  In 1960 it was officially called the Golden Shears competition which heralded the start of many similar, but much smaller, shearing competitions around the country. <i>(Mar 1, 1995,Capital Times,9</i>)

>  Mr Macdonald returned to Masterton a year later and rightfully claimed the Golden Shears crown, a feat he repeated in 1966. <i>(Jun 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  [Hdg] Ban on Golden Shears. New Zealand's top shearing event, the Golden Shears, is threatened by industrial action. <i>(Feb 12, 1977,Press, The,1</i>)

>  Fagan recently won his 15th Golden Shears title. The Golden Shears is the major shearing competition in this country. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an annual national shearing contest and festival held in late summer/early autumn at Masterton, Wairarapa.

>  Avery said his main focus now would be to win the Golden Shears. <i>(Oct 5, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  There has probably never been a more convincing winner of the Golden Shears open title ... <i>(Mar 22, 1979,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  Top New Zealand shearer David Fagan says he shocked himself as well as other people when he won the Golden Shears in Masterton las weekend. <i>(Mar 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  That was the week before the Golden Shears, so all hopes of winning went down the drain. <i>(Apr 6, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### golden snapper
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Marine) (See [snapper](sho-spa.md#snapper))

Distribution: Common, Bay of Islands and Great Barrier Island; taken as far south as Cape Campbell. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/12`</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i>Trachichthodes affinis </i> (Guenther). Golden snapper. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/406</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GOLDEN SNAPPER (KOAREA)<i> Centroberyx affinis </i> (Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,175</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE GOLDEN SNAPPER <i>Trachichthodes affinis (Gunther) </i>... Maori name: KOAREA. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Koarea ... Trachichthodes affinis, golden snapper </i>, a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,122</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED SNAPPER ... <i>Other names: Trachichthodes affinis;</i> golden snapper; nannygai (Australia); koarea (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,211</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Snapper, golden .. .. koarea .. .. <i>Trachichthodes affinis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> [RED SNAPPER] RED SNAPPER [<i>Trachichthodes affinis </i>] ... Also known as golden snapper, and koarea (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Rocky and sandy grounds, in the north, are the home of <i> Trachichthodes </i> ..., a golden fish known variously as golden snapper, koarea, and (in Australia) nannygai. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,215</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SNAPPER </i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Chrysophrys auratus </i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Golden Snapper (c) Other common names: Schnapper; Brim (small snapper at Nelson); Tamure. (d) Principal localities where taken: From Nelson northwards to both coasts of the North Island. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,45</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GOLDEN SNAPPER Not a snapper at all but a relative of the Roughy. This big-eyed, Golden (or Red) Snapper is usually taken accidentally when bottom fishing for snapper. <i>Ibid.</i> 161 A Golden Moki occurs off the East Coast of the South Island. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,114</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GOLDEN SNAPPER Centroberyx affinis (Trachichthodes affinis)</i> (Nannygai, red snapper, koarea) ... In spite of its name the golden snapper is not related to the true snapper and is in fact more similar to the roughys. ... Schools of golden snapper often move out over open sand flats ... and are then regularly caught in bottom trawls and sold as red fish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,177,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] red snapper .. [scientific name] <i>Centroberyx affinis </i>.. [other name(s)] golden snapper, nannygai, redfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Red snapper Trachichthodes affinis <i> Alternatively the golden snapper, and redfish or nannygai in southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,72</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] snapper, golden 1014-20 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Aciphylla aurea

### golden tainui
 
 <i>n.</i> a cultivated variety of tainui.

>  Golden Tainui or Kumarahou (Pomaderris kumeraho) - Another fast growing 2-3m high shrub, northern in origin, but certainly at home in Wellington's clay soils... Gum Diggers Soap is another name for this species. When water is added to leaves and rubbed in the hand, a lather forms. Seemingly utilised by the gum diggers of old to clean their hands. <i>(Nov 11, 1993,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Conspicuous in the basket, which was arranged by Miss Black, were Chatham Island lilies, clusters of Golden Tainui, red and white cleanthus, clematis, and yellow kowhai, most of which was obtained from Mr. O'Connor's property at Wilton's Bush. <i>(Oct 18, 1929,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The principal flowers in the wreath are the golden tainui, the yellow and white kowhai, the red and white kaka beak, the native orchid, broom, and honeysuckle, the wine-berry flower, and veronicas and young fern fronds and lawyers. <i>(Oct 18, 1933,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> But it's spring, and the parks are full of kowhai, and rangiora, and golden tainui ... <i>(1986,O.E.,28</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [<i>Podocarpus totara Aurea</i>]

### goldenbait
 
 <i>n.</i> the larvae of giant kokopu.

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Much less common is the banded kokopu (<i>Galaxias fasciatus</i>) sometimes known as Maori trout or native trout... Some whitebaiters refer to it as "golden bait" <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,13</i>)

>  .. - it's relatively simple to discern that the fish with their golden sheen (named "goldenbait" by fishers) are giant kokopu larvae .. Tipping the goldenbait and elephant ears back into the water will help ensure the survival of our endangered native fish. <i>(Aug 26, 2006,Dominion Post,E7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of whitebait

### goldenbush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>goldenbush n</i> 1 : a heathlike New Zealand shrub (<i>Cassinia fulvida</i>) of the family Compositae with evergreen yellowish foliage and white flowers <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,975</i>)

### goldfield
 
 <i>n.</i> . [goldmining; aka gold-field; incl attrib. Often specific with an identifying epithet (Thames Goldfield)]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We had, since the days of the Dunstan Rush, been accustomed to paying gold-field prices, and decided that it would take not less than five shillings [31] to satisfy our appetites. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,30</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Now, I, the Lieutenant-Governor of the Province of New Ulster, do hereby proclaim and declare that measures are now being taken with a view to that object, and that, as soon as the necessary arrangements shall be com-[156]pleted, Licenses to work the said Gold Field will be granted by the Government on payment of a reasonable Fee. <i>(1853,Auckland,155</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Nov 10 R.H.Wynyard, Lieut.-Governor Proclamation in 1853 Swainson Auckland 155]

>  Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852. ... the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: Heaphy's Report]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> It [Auckland] being ... about to become the nearest of our [sc. British] Australian posses-[vi]sions; a gold-field, moreover, having been recently discovered in its neighbourhood, the public may desire to learn some particulars of its history. <i>Ibid.</i> 93 ... and it was obvious to all that nothing but the united efforts of the Government, the colonists, and the natives, to carry out some well-considered system for the working of the gold-field, would prevent the discovery of gold in New Zealand from proving an unmitigated [94] curse. <i>Ibid.</i> 105 A few days after the sale [11 December 1852], the following Gold Circular was published, describing the quantity, character, and other particulars of the parcels of gold which had recently arrived from the [Thames] goldfield. <i>(1853,Auckland,v-vi</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> The Provincial Secretary: `It has not been gazetted yet, but it has been resolved upon as a goldfield.' <i>(1971,Wakatip,98</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Feb Hearing before Waste land Board in 1971 Griffiths King Wakatip 98]

>  ... I know the importance which belongs to news of any kind from these gold-fields, ... <i>(1861,Wakatip,5</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 20 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 5]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Commissioner was the title then given to the Gold-field's magistrates. ] <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/v/20,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> In the early days of any gold-field, or at `the first of the rush' as it is called, all is bustle and excitement. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,86</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... a petition was presented to the Council asking that a substantial reward should be offered for the discovery of a payable gold-field, and so prevent a further loss of population. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  goldfield n. a place where deposites of gold are known to exist. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> This conversation took place on the Westland gold-field in the spacious days of the gold-rush. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,48</i>)

### Goldfields Commissioner
 
 <i>n.</i> see Commissioner

### Goldfields Warden
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also Warden](../A#also-warden))

>  The Goldfields Warden ... for the Canterbury Government was kept busily employed marking off sections [in Hokitika]. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,6</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Interest grew into a fever of excitement ... the Gold Fields Warden hastened once more to the area, and the rumour stole abroad that the whole Island was to be declared a gold field. <i>(1940,Rakiura,234</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> Holidays could only be taken by decree of the goldfields warden .. <i>(1971,Wakatip,116</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> Holidays could only be taken by decree of the goldfields warden .. <i>(1971,Wakatip,116</i>)

>  Now, it must be remembered that a Goldfields Warden has large discretionary powers - for if he was to be guided by law, and law only, he would be unfit for his position, for the exigencies of a goldfield, especially on new rushes, calls forth the exercise of that valuable commodity called "common sense," though in many instances this may seem opposed to the strict reading of the law, therefore a Goldfields Warden is allowed some latitude in what may be called the equities of a case. <i>(May 13, 1879,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Mr Dalgleish, goldfields warden, died at Naseby this morning, after a short illness. <i>(May 9, 1900,Star, The,33</i>)

### goldfruit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Sir George Grey brought the grapefruit to New Zealand... It is thought that Grey's plant was either a pomelo, a sour orange hybrid, or a hybrid between the sour orange and the mandarin. Since 1981 these have been sold as "goldfruit". <i>(1985,Delectable Fruits Cookery,83</i>)

### goldie
 
 <i>n.</i> a sovereign (See [also little goldie = half sov](../A#also-little-goldie-=-half-sov))

>  Following are other local money-names...sovereign - thickun or goldie... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Bulletin, The (Sydney),(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Loafer , Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  Our nearness to Australia also resulted in our adoption of slang words in use there. Examples of these include goldie for sovereign, and little goldie for a half sovereign. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [used by Mrs Elsie Johns in NZ Oral History Unit, Martinborough study. she's a goldie she's ok; things are fine cf ?short for she's a gold watch ca1982 May p.c. Neil Rowe (Wellington), `She's a goldie' overheard from an old man]

>  <b>McGILL</b> goldie an impressive or well-regarded object <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [fish]

>  <b>DOAK</b> Skindivers and fishermen call the juvenile butterfish a `goldie' or `kelpie'. Many people will be surprised to learn that these two fishes are beyond doubt the same species. <i>(1972,Fishes,97</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldfish]

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> `Would a punch bowl do? A very big glass one?' Sidney asks. `Not for the tropicals. A couple of goldies - ' ... `You said you wanted a goldfish.' <i>(1985,Married Alive,98</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a goldfinch

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> Trapping goldfinches ... - but finches brought a shilling a pair. ... The birds one snared in triumph [as a boy] brought their own punishment. The `goldies' one put in the soap box ... looked so small and helpless. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,225</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Universal, archive. aka gold pan, gold-pan; goldmining]

### golf-stick moss
 
 <i>n.</i>

### gonce
 
 <i>n.</i> Money, cash. [(obs)]

>  The typewriter girl has nothing to do with the gonce in her bosses [sic] safe, although she manipulates his keys. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/1/1</i>)

>  He was after land an' gonce an' boodle ... <i>(Oct 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

>  If the port is full of shipping / I'm so anxious for the `gonce'; / But the Union will not let me / Work all the ships at once. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,42</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### gone a million
 
 <i>phr.</i> dead [(prison slang)..]

>  Mafish.- Sam Slang remarks; It is well known how the Turkish word `mafish' is commonly used now in place of old slang, such as `gone a million,' `blown out,' `snuffed it,' `clenched his fist,' `crashed in,' `handed in his checks,' `took the count,' `skied the towel,' etc. <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,15</i>)

### gone by lunchtime
 
 <i>phr.</i> swiftly removed (as a political expedient) [orig. Don Brash re getting rid of nuke-free policy.]

>  This is the meeting at which Brash is recorded as uttering the now deathless phrase "Gone by lunchtime" in connection with the anti-nuclear legislation. <i>(Aug 13, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

### goneburger
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>v.</i> spit

 <i>n.</i> [1984 17 M E106Pakuranga Coll. 3 GOOBER]

 <i>n.</i> [usu. schoolboys')]

How could he know that word, he was too young? Why, it wasn't even in the Heinemann New Zealand Dictionary. Sometimes it meant something worse, vis-a[grave]-vis nose.
