### good as gold
 
 <i>exclam.</i> as an expression of agreement, approval, etc. [ca1988 Jan 19 p.c. Mrs Rowe (a recent arrival from Britain) `used in the same way in south Cornwall (c1987); as is crib `lunch']

>  good as gold! inf. excellent!, very good! <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,213</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Meet Les Smith,' said the driver. `Gidday,' said Les Smith. `How's tricks, then, Bert? Good trip?' `No trouble, Les.' `Good as gold,' said the helmsman. <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,36</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> everything's fine, right as rain [From N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html 1986 L.Bauer cp general English - more literal The baby didn't cry all night. She was good as gold.]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> I won't spare money or fires. I'll be as good as gold and May will be divine. 264 [dateline incomplete] There, you can keep on your pins, can't you? Yeah, good as gold. You'll be set. <i>(Nov 11, 1919,Letters</i>) [NOTES: 1919 Nov 11 Mansfield Letters (1951 Murry) 385]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> [affirmation] `The men get a good breakfast?' asked the Colonel. `Good as gold, sir. I tried it myself.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,77</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> `Hello, Alf. Are we all right parked here?' `Yeah; good as gold. Have you seen Ed Cairns?' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,133</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> We do not know whether an affable New Zealander about to agree to something is going to say extra good or good as gold or simply all right or even O.K. [177]Recent popular expressions are good as gold or extra good, both meaning simply `good'. Good as gold is an affable way of saying `Right you are'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,30, 177</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Not stiff after yesterday's game are you? said the Coach solicitously. Good as gold, said the Captain. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,52</i>)

>  <b>McDERMOTT</b> Again we had called ahead for accommodations, received the `chock-a-block' reply and referral to another motel and got a `good-as-gold' confirmation. <i>(1976,How to Get Lost & Found in NZ,140</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ... he pulls out the Barrett standard weekly order. ... `There you go, Phil. Good as gold.' <i>(1979,Skin Deep,93</i>)

>  A bulldozer operator, Alicetown, told the `Post' he was `as good as gold' after surviving an accident where his vehicle rolled on top of him.... <i>(Feb 16, 1984,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> 'Hunky dory?' 'Right as rain. Good as gold,' I said, setting off down the corridor. It occurred to me that alliteration plays a considerable part in the formation of our idioms. <i>(2009,Living As A Moon,242</i>)

>  Then there is the fascinating and still puzzling way that Maori and Pakeha appear to be making a new sort of society, in spite of petty politics and pork barrelling, with the vibrancy of the Pacific injecting an added bravura. As they say here, constantly, good as gold. <i>(Sep 3, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),C3</i>)

### good as grass
 
 <i>phr.</i> (as) good as grass; a proverbial catchphrase for anything very good, or to indicate agreement etc; formed prob, by analogy of the older As good as gold (see Apperson, 1843). -->ca1910 (Marlborough and elsewhere) eg. The baby's been as good as grass while you were away. `Well, you agree then?' `It's as good as grass' [= `I agree perfectly'] -->nf Apperson.

### good burn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We had just had what is called a "good burn". <i>(Feb 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  It was he who had just had a "good burn." <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### good cutting
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [sheep] easy to shear.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> They are big and well-wooled but will be good cutting. Bluey and I were determined to try and equal his tally if we got some good cutting sheep. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,75</i>)

### good doer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] a person who eats a lot but is productive and efficient.

>  Good doer: a person who may be overweight but works hard. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He's taken me out for dinner a couple of times and is very impressed with my appetite... He'd said admiringly that I was "a good doer". <i>(Sep 13, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that converts feed to meat or milk readily or that performs well when put to test.

>  It is said that the farmer ought only to buy a horse that is a good 'doer' - that is to say a horse that does justice to his food. If a horse, instead of putting on muscle under wholesome treatment puts on fat, that horse is not a good, but a 'bad doer.' <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,236</i>)

>  <b>KAY</b> Sheep raised on coastal land facing into the wind off the sea were regarded as good-doers. <i>(1996,Anthony Wall, Settler of Porirua,98</i>)

>  .. some milking-strains being more susceptible than others; the improved cow - the heavy milker, the good doer - is the most likely to suffer, and high-conditioned animals are all predisposed to milk-fever. <i>(Dec 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,467</i>)

>  A 'good doer' is an animal which makes the most valuable use of the food which it consumes .. robust health is essential in a 'good doer'... <i>(Aug 5, 1936,Weekly News,78</i>)

>  Having established in your mind's eye the class of sheep you require for your conditions, the next step should be to ensure that they are 'good do'ers.' A bright eye is another indicator of a good do'er. <i>(Jan 1, 1941,NZ Dairy Exporter,42</i>)

>  We find the Friesians better doers in Northland. <i>(Jan 11, 1989,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  I've got cows who weren't such good doers, but out of the stress of a bigger herd they've blossomed. [good do'er; better doer] <i>(Apr 19, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### good faith bargaining
 
 <i>n.</i> [term introduced c1999 for Labour plans for union legislation]

>  The <i>piece de resistance</i> of the new Bill is good faith bargaining. <i>(Mar 29, 2000,Independent (Auckland),28</i>)

>  Bosses and unions have to be nice to each other or else, under new "good faith"legislation... <i>Ibid.</i> 17 "Good faith" is the most mandatory thing, and it is not exhaustively defined. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The legislation provides that, at the least, good-faith bargaining requires the union and employer to use their best endeavours to sort out a bargaining process in an effective and efficient manner... <i>(Apr 5, 2000,Dominion,27</i>)

>  The words "good faith bargaining" are as devoid of meaning as the sounds you hear in a seashell. <i>(Mar 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  What's the "just" result when someone breaches a contract? What is "good faith bargaining"? It has been left up to the judges. <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  However, the assumption that "good-faith bargaining" makes for a happier, more productive workplace is contentious. <i>(Aug 2, 2000,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Productive employment relationships were seen as relationships founded on good faith, and for the first time in New Zealand, the statute spelled out in detail what behaving in "good faith" in an employment relationship might look like... Good faith" obligations are again used as the principal mechanism to deliver more productive employment relationships. <i>(Jul 21, 2004,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### good gold
 
 <i>n.</i> plentiful gold [goldmining]

>  Waitahuna and the Woolshed are progressing well. On the latter many parties are reported to be getting "good gold." <i>(Nov 12, 1861,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> There were over a thousand diggers ... waiting to see the result of the prospect when we bottomed. We ... struck very good gold ... We built a paddock, rigged up a windlass, and got several very good washings out of this shaft ... <i>Ibid.</i> 64 There was no ground to be got there. ... There was good gold there, but the claims were all taken up. <i>Ibid.</i> 142 From there [Molyneux] I went to Gabriel's Gully, where I opened a claim and got good gold. ' <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,56</i>)

### good ink
 
 <i>n.</i> (someone or something) good, agreeable, pleasant [Esp. that's good ink.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Good ink(, that's). (That is) good, agreeable, pleasant: New Zealanders': from ca. 1910. Cf. good pup. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,342</i>)

>  ... `good pup' and `good ink' are terms of approval. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The,4</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Twentieth century ... expressions ... include good ink, something pleasant or agreeable <i>(1941,NZ Slang,57</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> good ink agreeable; eg `He's the good ink, that lad. Heart of gold, slaves his guts out, no trouble to anyone.' Obs. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)

### good keen bloke
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [good keen man](go-gra.md#good-keen-man))

>  Harold Blair, curator and panel beater, was the Good Keen Bloke of Wellington's light aircraft fraternity. <i>(Jul 1, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Given his large natural charisma and eccentricity, Crump swiftly became almost inseparable from his books. A slavish media turned to him whenever there was a need for a good keen bloke kind of character., Crump could always be counted on to be himself in cinemascope. <i>(Oct 29, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F1</i>)

### good keen man
 
 <i>n.</i> [1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 8 KEEN MAN (aj + KEEN MAN)]

>  Even couch potatoes can sometimes still see themselves as a good keen man or woman. <i>(Oct 16, 1993,Dominion,17</i>)

>  Todd Blackadder is the original good keen man from Barry Crump's book. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,78</i>)

>  Burton Shipley may have quit farming for a desk job, but he proved yesterday he is still a good keen man at heart. <i>(Sep 13, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Good Keen Man shone in a generous crowd. <i>(Feb 18, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> [Title] A Good Keen Man. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSON</b> I didn't see how he could possibly have rushed across a couple of hundred yards of mountain, barefoot ... Bill was the original good keen man all right. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,30</i>)

>  The Real Man of this new quiche legend is a variant of our old friend, the Good Keen Man. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  After all, if your car broke down on the Desert Road, at least the Good Keen Man would stop and help you out. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> The evolution of the Kiwi bloke has been one of rapprochement and training of the wild beats within - taking the 'hard' out of the good, keen, hard man. <i>(1991,Kiwi,53</i>)

>  [Harold Wellman] might have been British, but in many ways he was an early exemplar of the Good Keen Man. <i>(Sep 3, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> "You'll get used to Keith... I reckon he's the original good keen man." <i>(1971,Pack & Rifle,69</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1986 edition]

>  <b>WOLFE</b> A good keen man, first published in 1960, could be described as the bloke's bible. <i>(1991,Kiwi,61</i>)

>  And the good keen men left a more palpable legacy after firing their last rounds: Spartan shelters of wood and corrugated iron, and tracks marked with tin lids and bits of venetian blind. <i>(Jan 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

>  And that Barry Crump turned out to be an impotent wife-beater in now way invalidates the "Good Keen Man" as a cultural icon. <i>(Apr 10, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

### good King Henry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> GOOD KING HENRY (<i>Chenopodium bonushenricus</i>) ... This plant was advocated by King Henry VIII as a vegetable in times of scarcity ...; The popular name ..., fat Henry was abbreviated to fathen and later applied to the common, related weed. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,1</i>)

### good Kiwi bloke
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Kiwi bloke]

>  A good Kiwi bloke: Jerry Mataparae consulted his wife, Janine, before accepting his new role, though she says "it was a very easy decision." <i>(Mar 9, 2011,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  He hated Good Kiwi Blokes : "The ones that can take a bit of 4 x 2, a couple of rusty nails and an empty baked bean can and build an emergency weather balloon out of it when required. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  He was one of those "good Kiwi blokes" who brought fun, laughter and magic to many people during his lifetimes [sic]. <i>(Apr 16, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### good lamber
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe that does not require assistance during lambing.

>  <b>HALL</b> And when they are not disturbed they are naturally good lambers. The poor lambers don't survive and that's all there is to it. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,16</i>)

### good mark
 
 <i>n.</i> a trustworthy person [AND good/bad mark n1 1835] [1845. R. Howitt, `Australian,' `I wondered often what was the meaning of this, amongst many other peculiar colonial phrases, `Is the man a good mark?' I heard it casually from the lips of apparently respectable setters, as they rode on the highways, `Such and such a one is a good mark,' simply a person who pays his men their wages, without delays or draw-backs; a man to whom you may sell anything safely; for there are in the colony people who are regularly summoned before the magistrates by every servant they employ for wages. They seem to like to do everything publicly, legally, and so become notoriously not `good marks.'' ca1941 He's OK. A good mark up to about 50 quid ca1949 You're lucky to get him for the job. He's a good mark.]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mark, a good,</i> Australian slang <i>(1898,Austral English,287</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> He's one of the best-marks in Timbertown <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,121</i>)

>  <i>mark n.</i>-a good mark sl. an honest or trustworthy person. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <i> mark, a good n.(slang).</i> reliable, honest, trustworthy person: also oppositely `bad mark'. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>mark, a good n. (Sl.)</i> reliable, honest, trustworthy person: <i>bad mark</i>, the opposite. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

### good night, McGuiness
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (See [DNZE goodbye](../D#dnze-goodbye),[McGinnis](../M#mcginnis))

>  <b>McGILL</b> Good night, McGuiness! a rather final version of the evening farewell salutation. McGuiness not known; eg `Ah, well, that's one more than my usual quota. That's it lads. It's good night, McGuiness. See ya in the morning, maybe.' c. 1910. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,1952</i>)

### good oil
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [oil](o-on.md#oil))

### good on you/good on ya
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) well done! good for you!

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `Good on you, Uncle Ted,' says Jake. `You're not too old yet.' <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,151,1979 ed.</i>)

>  The following I have noted since I came to New Zealand ... `good on you': equivalent - good for you, with a definite emphasis [in British English] on the last word. <i>(Dec 1, 1957,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: W.J. Jimpson Letter (Tolaga Bay)]

>  You bring back good memories. Good on you, mate. <i>(Oct 15, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  By the end, it'll seem as though the rose clamped in your mate's teeth was always there, and you'll join the rest nodding "Good on ya, mate." <i>(Mar 15, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

### good on you/onya
 
 <i>exclam.</i> an exclamation of encouragement, agreement (contrast gen.English good f'you) [ca1940 Havelock; also good on him etc.]

>  I lower my voice to a low rumble: "Good on ya, mate." <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/34</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Good on you, boy,' said Alleyn, displaying what he [173] hoped was the correct idiom and the proper show of enthusiasm.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,172</i>)

>  good ... 34. good on you. Austral. & N.Z. well done, well said, etc.: a term of congratulation. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,479</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 324; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 213]]

>  <b>CASEY</b> `So I suppose that means I'll have to come with you.' `Oh, good on you!' <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,211</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Well done! A combination of encouragement and approval. .in 4;`So Mac trounced the big fat chap and Mac's only about up to his armpit! Well, good on him!' -->1966 Turner English Language in Australia and NZ 11 I have heard it suggested that we owe the fine friendly Australian `good on you' (pronounced always with the emphasis on the word on) to an Irish blessing, and that the vulgar dyke for `urinal' is used in Ireland, ... <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],441,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> `Good on you! Let us know if there is anything we can do to help.' <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,130</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> My father's speech to his constituents was punctuated with shouts of `Good on you!' `Good on you!' `Tell us some more!' <i>(1968,Seddons, The,60</i>)

>  He was delighted to hear that a Whangarei group was considering joining in on the beer stike. `Good on them. ....', he said. <i>(Jan 30, 1970,Dominion,2/1</i>)

>  `Good on yer!' Old dig Alf Allen on a tough job well done. Pitchforked into the Speaker's chair ..., Alf faced the unenviable task of warding off chaos in the House. <i>(Oct 21, 1972,Sports Post,8/4</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> good on yer/you: a term of approbation. Means, roughly, GOOD FOR YOU <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,47</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> good on you Good for you. F.E. Dickie's Snow in Summer (1967) page 15. <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

>  `Good on you,' said Chatterton, and they lifted their forks in mutual salutation. <i>(Jul 25, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>) [NOTES: Marshall A Day With Yestermen]

>  <b>GRAY</b> Maybe she doesn't ... and good on her. <i>(1987,Stepping Out,61</i>)

>  `Come on! It's late!' Good on you Terry! <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,132</i>) [NOTES: (Morrissey Terry Loves me)]

>  <b>CHISHOLME</b> Some people criticised him for speaking up, but good on him. <i>(1987,From the Heart,32</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> good on ya/yer/you matey expression of approval; eg `Good on you, sport, you're a bloody bewdy, bringing round the chainsaw. Promise I'll get it back in one or three pieces. No sweat.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)

>  (Kiwi Lingo) [British] Good for you [NZ] good on yu[sic] ... [Bri.] Royal greeting [NZ] brown eye <i>(Jun 10, 1989,Dominion,10</i>)

>  "I thought, yeah, good onya, Tana. ..." <i>(Oct 4, 2005,NZ Herald,C14</i>)

### good one
 
 <i>exclam.</i> Used very frequently in various contexts to express approval, or, ironically, disapproval.

### good pup
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> good pup. Anything good, e.g. a successful sale, a good bargain, a comfortable dug-out: New Zealanders': C. 20. Prob. at first a farmers' c.p. of commendation. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,342</i>)

>  . . .`good pup' and `good ink' are terms of approval. <i>(Apr 4, 1983,Press, The,4</i>) [NOTES: McNab N Z Slang]

### good sport
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> [the New Zealander] loves sport of all kinds and is what is known as a `good sport' in every way <i>(1927,Empire's Junior Partner, The,236</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Used in 3rd person. A woman who does not maintain a proper standard of morals in her social life. The reference is not necessarily derogatory, but factual. `Sara's a good sport. She won't disappoint you.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],976,974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLDER</b> <i>(1933,Restless Youth,104</i>)

>  <b>BELT</b> `My daughter is a very good sport,' she said. They left the hotel together. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,72</i>)

### good thing
 
 <i>n.</i> Often she's a good thing. A good, profitable or successful investment, piece of business, venture etc, or one potentially, and fairly certainly, good etc; esp. make a good thing of ..., be on to a good thing. [1960 esp. in expression be on to a good thing of something (a job, a lurk, a girl) probably from racing usage 2. -->ca1938 (Havelock) -->ca1950 (Wellington) -->c. too much of a good thing. [Cf OED Too 5b Too much of a good thing quot. 1809 Syd. Smith Works (1867) `This (to use a very colloquial phrase) is surely too much of a good thing'. So P., and also quoting Apperson: Shakespeare As You Like It V-1 for a literal sense: Apperson also quotes Cotgrave 1611, and Syd. Smith. P8:487 Br : to be pursuing something advantageous.] Usu. in negative; cf You can't have too much ..., or, humorously, of a person who has had too much to drink etc: he's had too much ... ->ca1945 (Wellington: students) -->ca1960 (Auckland)]

>  <b>BRIGHT</b> .. poor folks ... all flocking to `make a good thing of it'. <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,129</i>)

>  Sewell pulled up and does not let any more [sections] on the same terms so that a few who applied first have got a good thing. <i>(1853,Canterbury Papers,72</i>) [NOTES: [JE Fitgerald, Chch]]

>  ... communicate directly with Ryan ... who wants a mate in a good thing, and would be a useful partner at the Royal. <i>(Sep 30, 1865,Dunedin Punch,133</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> ... he made quite a good thing out of it. <i>(1895,South Sea Siren,165</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A sound enterprise, a pleasing situation. The speaker often proceeds to give grounds for his optimism. .in 4;`It's a good thing! With luck we'll finish all that the boss wants in the morning, and the river's handy; we'll get in at least two, or even three hours fishing.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],444</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> good thing, eh!, `you can't beat it!' etc. as an affirmation.



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [P: racing slang a `presumed certainty' (-1884).] A cert; a sure winner, or sure tip for a winner; a good horse. Esp. be, get on (to) a good thing.

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> Tippings was the repository of all the `good things' which ever found their way into this quiet and remote settlement. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,519</i>)

>  They told me there was some good things to be picked up [at the Trotting meeting]. ... I always did like to get onto a good thing. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,38</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> good thing: Terry was on a good thing, Terry backed a winner. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary]

### good tucker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) healthy pasture feed.

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> There were always little bits of wet ground and boggy places where there was a bit of good tucker and the heavy, full-woolled sheep would get stuck in these places ... <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,36</i>)

### good way on, a
 
 <i>phr.</i> having made good progress

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To have made very fair progress. 'He's a good way on with it, he's making good progress. I should say in another week he'll have finished the outside, porch, verandah and all.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],449,1974 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1962-62]

### good-mouth
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a full-mouth sheep [attrib. use below] (See [full-mouth](fly-fuz.md#full-mouth))

>  Good-mouth ewes and lambs changed hands at up to 59/- <i>(Nov 8, 1951,NZ Farmer,42</i>)

### good-oh
 
 <i>adv.</i> As an adverb intensifying the action of the verb, `like billy-oh'.

>  <b>ANZAC</b> The rain came down good-oh. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,34</i>)

>  ... beltin' her about goodo. <i>(Oct 31, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,133</i>)

>  The week-end boys from Trentham and Featherston [military camps], more especially the see oners, were after these birds good O. <i>(Apr 29, 1925,NZ Tit-Bits,6</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> [A policemen says] I tears into [the passageway] good-oh, and I come at it that determined-like I swept all before me .... <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,165</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i> very well (? influenced by semantics of good). [?Can this be separated from 1a]

>  We tried her [the machine] out till we was sick and tired of her and she worked corker every time. She worked good-oh, didn't she. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,49</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The drummer, who has lost his uniform cap, ... [is] wearing a hard-knocker hat. He glares hard and bangs out the bumpy waltz time good-oh. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> an exclamation or indication of assent, agreement `all right, then'; right-oh; she's right, etc.

>  <b>PEACOCKE</b> `Goodo', said Dal. <i>(1926,His Kid Brother,158</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Good-oh, ... get to it, you boys'. It is extraordinary how ubiquitous [New Zealanders] make that remark. It expresses anything from acquiescence to approbation. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,61</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Australianisms] Goodoh and rightoh are used by everyone, even our savants. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>HOLLAND & LILIENTHAL</b> Goodo! : fine, OK <i>(1942,Meet the Anzacs,14</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The exclamation `good!' has become `goodo!', on the analogy of `righto!'. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> `Good-o,' replied the other [New Zealander]. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,42</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The words righto and goodo are not confined to Australia and New Zealand but are so prevalent here that they deserve mention. <i>(1996,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,135</i>)

>  good-oh or good-o interj. Brit. & Austral. inf. an exclamation of pleasure, agreement, etc. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,479</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 324; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 213]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> good-oh exclamation of approval or agreement; variant of right-oh; eg `Adds up, does it, Mr B? Yes? Good-oh, then. See you next week. Bye.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> all right, very good

>  <b>THOMSON</b> That was real good-o. We `diddled' them properly that time. Didn't Biggins come a `bouncer', eh ? <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,34</i>)

>  good oh a. fine, excellent, bonzer. [origin uncertain] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... goodoh: certainly, O.K. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> They thought it was good-oh the first night, even the fish and chips was fun, ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,13</i>)

>  <b>HAR VEY</b> `Yo have mah permission to take another shillin'.' This was good-o all right. So I took another half-crown ... <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,34</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> `O.K., Allan, that'll be good-oh. It's never too early for me. ...' <i>(1964,High Country Family,41</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Then got a blanket. Coupla books. By then all the fellas gone. Day-shift jokers, y'know. So goodoh to beaut there [sunbathing].' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,135</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HARVEY</b> I'll probably end up as a major or something good-o. Something with plenty of perks. That's me. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,110</i>)



 
 <i>adv.</i> well

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> After all that, the special mungaree she was given began to show results: the little girl of Wadi Hanein started thriving goodoh. <i>(1990,Soldier Country,126</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Candlegrease came off my shirt or shorts goodoh when mum went to work with brown paper and a hot iron. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,115</i>)

>  ...Took the pain away, antiseptic apparently, healed goodoh. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,71</i>)

>  The next thing Day saw was witness and her "hubby" strafing each other "good-o." <i>(Dec 29, 1917,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### good-shifting
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sale stock] not well-fed, but will thrive on better land.

>  Top Romney flock ... Great shifting, farmed on hummocky, stoney country at Opunake. <i>(Dec 22, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,27</i>)

>  Dairy herd for sale. 1 August 2002 calving. Good shifting cattle. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,24</i>)

>  Good shifting deer to suit all purchasers. [great shifting] <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Dominion,33</i>)

### good-uddered
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of cows] possessing strong, full udders with good conformation.

### goodbye and bugger you

### goodbyee
 
 <i>exclam.</i> An occasional form (rather affected) of goodbye. P. quotes Collinson `A c.p. form, ca 1915-20, of goodbye!', but in NZ attested at least as late as: 1934 Mrs Lusk Waimana 82 `goodbyee and kia-ora'; heard in Wellington (1952) and Wairarapa (1953); and, if memory serves correctly, was either the title or refrain of a song during WWI. [? omit]

### goodie
 
 <i>n.</i> a good one

>  That was a goodie - there was a fair bit of elation after that. <i>(Jul 14, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  goodie n. (slang) good person or article. Similarly baddie. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  goodie n. (Sl.) good person or article. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

### goodletite
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) a rock type discovered in Westland (named after Wally Goodlet)

>  The striking ruby-rich rock from the West Coast commonly called "goodletite", was first discovered as a boulder by a miner near Rimu a little over one hundred years ago. <i>(Jul 1996,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,110/33</i>)

>  In a passage on Goodlet, one of the lines is "Goodletite we'll always call it." <i>(Jul 1996,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,110/33</i>)

>  Who could guess at "Goodletite", for instance, a rock studded with delicate rubies that is named after Black's assistant, Wally Goodlet, and found only in Westland? <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/31</i>)

>  Goodletite is an informal name applied to a rock consisting or [sic] ruby corundum and green mica found as boulders during gold mining operation near Hokitika (NZGS Bull. 6, p. 123, 1908) <i>(Jun 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,84/66</i>)

>  As Brian comments, the source of the goodletite is unknown as no in situ outcrop has ever been located. <i>(Aug 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,85/31</i>)

### goodnight
 
 <i>exclam.</i> As a greeting [[? omit] -]

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> he bade his host and hostess [homeys ?] `goodnight' on entering the room, which disconcerted them temporaraily until they realised he meant it as a greeting not as farewell. -->(W.Ramson comments: this is current Aust: I'd not struck it in NZ) <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,136</i>)

### Goodnight Kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> the signing-off character for many years on Television New Zealand

>  Dame Malvina Major and Howard Morrison's rendition of "Hine e Hine" at a Wellington Concert prompted [her] to ask whether this was the tune that accompanied the "goodnight kiwi" as he climbed up the tower to bed at the end of TV transmission for the night a few years back. <i>(May 24, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Pete was lying on the couch watching the TVNZ Goodnight Kiwi climb into his satellite dish. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  But then those were the days when people would stay up just to watch the Goodnight Kiwi go to bed on top of the TV mast. <i>(Jul 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> [also as goodbye McGinnis]

 <i>n.</i> Elaboration of doover, doofer, gadget; thingumebob; any small object. [ca1951 used Mr King P&T Engineer, Port Fitzroy R.T. Station Mr. P.McGeedy, Whangaparapara Mr Deane, NZ Forest Service, Fitzroy.]

### goog
 
 <i>n.</i> an egg [ca1941 St Patrick's, Silverstream. slang]

>  goog, googy n. an egg, whence full as a goog, drunk. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> goog an antipodean egg, often a boiled one; probably from a toddler attempt, or a contraction of `Good egg'. - an idiot. Both NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Short for a fig. golden goog, golden egg: a sovereign.

>  Mug is a humorous sort, and seen after the race in question, he remarked, `She has cost me about 60 googs', but I'll take my oath the squib will not cost me 61 Not another `deener.' [punctuation sic] <i>(Feb 27, 1909,NZ Truth,193/2/8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Shortened or altered form of goosegog, a gooseberry. [ca1938 Havelock]

### googie
 
 <i>n.</i> a sovereign

>  Therefore, peace, be still; let Chapman and Co. gather up the sunbeams - in the shape of golden googies, silver and copper coins - scattered splentifully around their path, as Sankey and other Yankee-doodling hymn-howlers used to bawl when over here gathering in the gonce along with Brudder Moody. <i>(Jul 24, 1909,NZ Truth,213/1/2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an egg (See [also goog](go-gra.md#goog))

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Mrs Daniels calls eggs googies. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,32</i>)

### google factory

### googly
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Indeed, Jake has a hard job to appear polite and attentive, because Mabel ... makes googly eyes behind Mr Moss ... <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,110,1979 ed.</i>)

>  And she [Mrs Heta] was always reneging in five hundred! But her greatest asset was her eyes, which were big and googly. One eye would look straight ahead, while the other swivelled around, having a look at the cards in the hands of the women sitting next to her. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,160</i>) [NOTES: 1972 Ihimaera `Game of Cards']

### googly-eyed cod
 
 <i>n.</i> ribaldo, also called deepsea cod.

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,16</i>) [NOTES: See cit. deepsea cod.]

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>RIBALDO</i> (Googly-eyed cod) <i>Mora moro (Mora pacifica) </i>... The ribaldo is a plump-bodied cod. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,144,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Ribaldo Mora moro</i> ... Alternatively googley-eyed cod. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,58</i>)

### gook
 
 <i>n.</i> A Korean Asian. [(orig. and mainly services') Cf hoon. (Cf 1951 Jan 1 Time 16: gook used frequently.) a. ca1951 F.M. O'Brien used by tramwaymen, from `K-force Yakker']

>  No, no, not Aryan, it is in disgrace:/ Caucasian's now the word for `master race'./ Let gook, wog, nigger and such lesser folk/ Admit the claim of New World herrenvolk. <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,19</i>)

>  It is the arrogance of the American labelling other peoples `gooks' and `wops': the New Zealand soldier had his `Wogs' and `Ities' and `Teds', at home there are the `Chinks' and `Ikes' and `Dallies' and even the `Horis'. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23: 220</i>) [NOTES: Pearson `Fretful Sleepers']

>  [a Korean Asian] ... they [sc. films] usually depict an organising enemy either sinisterly Asian or vaguely East European ... It is no big step ... to dropping napalm on `gooks'. <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,34</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> [a Korean] `Me and my mob were on patrol ... when we run into this crowd of gooks.' ... `Why was that gook patrol even operating?' <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,316</i>)

### goolie
 
 <i>n.</i> A rock, stone.

>  BRUCE BYRNE 54. An Australian from Nhill in Victoria and resident locally for about 15 years. Worked for Greytown Borough Council. `I w's scared at first, diggin' graves. Now I lie in 'em for size!' Introduced a new word into the District - `Goulie' for the large stones that abound in the local soil. [M. Burton's note for H.NZDQ (May 1984)] `The word `goulie' was a colloquialism in Nhill and is now common among council workers in Greytown.' <i>(1977,South Wairarapa Workingmen's Club Centennial Book</i>) [NOTES: Greytown 1976]

>  Port Waikato - river mouth. Whitebaiting ? ... rock in tin of whitebait to increase weight when selling? '... I kept on pouring it into my containers and there was a dirty big goolie on the bottom.' <i>(Oct 18, 1982,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Oct 18 2YA Wellington 9.25. [?]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> goolie a stone or rock on the West Coast; in Australia it is a stone specifically suited to throwing; origins may be in `gull' or `gully', terms used in marbles, Partridge ponders. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large rock.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Goolie: a large rock. Goolies make excellent Nelson huntaways and are pretty good at spoiling ploughs. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,36</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> .. too light [a charge] and it would only crack the rock without releasing any 'goolies.' <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kumara](kiw-kwi.md#kumara))

 <i>n.</i>

### goon squad
 
 <i>n.</i> a semi-illegal heavy mob to keep prisoners in order

>  Yet, an employment court in Christchurch recently heard that Paparua Prison housed an emergency response team (also known as the goon squad) that is supposed to have harassed inmates during night-time raids, and which included the army and police buddies of squad members. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  To describe the Canterbury Goon Squad as having developed a culture (Dec 18) is a gross misuse of the word. <i>(Dec 23, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  [Hdg] Court told of goon squad's macho culture... He denied that the act reflected the macho culture engulfing the now disbanded unit, better known as the goon squad, but did not explain further... The hearing lifted the lid on how the shadowy goon squad operated ror nearly a year up to June 2000. <i>(May 21, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Goon squad' unmasked... Mr French confirmed early allegations that bizarre and intimidatory tactics were used by the shadowy security team - dubbed the goon squad - to provoke a reaction from inmates. <i>(Jul 14, 2002,Press, The</i>)

>  Operation Blitzkrieg, the codename for one of its secret missions, sums up the short life of the Canterbury prisons' "Goon Squad"... Prison lobby group the Howard League for Penal Reform is calling for a ministerial inquiry into the Goon Squad and how it was formed without proper controls... "The Goon Squad leaders took it upon themselves to adopt absolute power and it went to their heads." <i>(May 29, 2002,Dominion,16</i>)

>  A convicted murderer is suing the Corrections Department for $150,000 over alleged rough treatment by the renegade prison "goon squad"... The goon squad was set up as the country's first full-time response team, with considerable resources - but without a business plan, proper management or accountability regimes. <i>(Sep 25, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  A convicted murderer is suing the Corrections Department for $150,000 over alleged rough treatment by the renegade prison "goon squad"... The goon squad was set up as the country's first full-time response team, with considerable resources - but without a business plan, proper management or accountability regimes. <i>(Sep 25, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  Police say they have decided not to charge the former head of the so-called goon squad, the notorious now-defunct prison emergency response unit. <i>(Jun 7, 2002,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] No charge for goon squad boss. <i>(Jun 7, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)

### goop
 
 <i>v.</i> to stare, gawp

>  <b>SLATTER</b> When the Waimak floods in December twenty thousand dam the roads to goop at it. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,52</i>)

### goopity
 
 <i>adj.</i> like goop

### goori fight
 
 <i>n.</i> a dogfight; transf. to an insignificant contest

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> You can tell its no more than a goorie fight to him. He don't care which side wins. <i>(1942,Sweet Beulah Land,57</i>)

### goorie/goory
 
 <i>n.</i> a poorly-performing racehorse [Common in Taranaki in 1950s (DEH)]

>  <b>T. O'SULLIVAN</b> `That goory would have its work cut out to come last.' <i>(1920,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>W. MABBET</b> <i>(1949,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: `That thing's [sc a race-horse] only a goory.']



 
 <i>n.</i> variant of kuri (See [kuri](kiw-kwi.md#kuri))

>  An irredeemable ratbag was a "filthy goory". <i>(Aug 11, 2004,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> goory Pakeha corruption of `kuri', Maori for dog, usually used as abusive term for a person, or a dog; eg `Get that goory off my property quick smart, or I'll call the cops.' <i>(1988,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,52</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The goories scavenged about the neighbours' gardens and cocked their legs against the gateposts. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,82</i>) [NOTES: 1949 quote]

>  Soon as I tied up the goory,.. / I'll be livin' it high and livin' it hard. <i>(1967,Shanties by the Way,148</i>)

### goose
 
 <i>n.</i> Often used in NZ for the naturalised Canada goose Branta canadensis.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Canadian geese were in their thousands ... <i>(1952,Deer Shooter,35</i>)

>  Increased bag limits have been announced for Black Swans, Paradise Shellduck and Canadian Geese in the central North Island this hunting season. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... That difficult customer with whom the salesman can never clinch the deal is known as a `goose'. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,2/1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [G.J.Griffiths notes: connotations of sexual or homosexual intercourse, but used as schoolboys in Invercargill in 1930s, 40s meaning holding hands together, as if for prayer, and jabbing the fingers into backside and especially the tail-bone - perhaps this was simply the innocent version of the adult action. -->1971 Aug 4 R.K.Emerson [?] used it in conver]



 
 <i>v.</i> To scold; to dress down, cf goss. [G. Griffiths: ?thwart, or trick.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> My, eye, didn't [the jailer] `goose' them. [check cit and sense] <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also solan goose (=gannet)](sho-spa.md#solan-goose)))



 
 <i>n.</i> [sawmilling]

>  <b>MANDER</b> They watched the progress of the log from the booms up the skids to the side of the breakdown platform... Halved and quartered, it was then levered ... on to greased rollers and rushed towards the big circulars, which turned it into flitches. Then the small circulars, the drag, and the goose completed its metamorphosis into the regulation strips ... - <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,198,1960 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> An early name =paradise duck (the bird is really a goose (Andersen Maori Words 1946)). [Get cits from DUCK, PARADISE DUCK.]

>  <b>STOKES</b> Wikas and paradise geese. <i>(1851,Survey of Sthn Part of Middle Isd,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  (Torlesse.) ... the Paradise Duck, or New Zealand variegated goose. <i>(Jul 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (See [colonial goose](chi-con.md#colonial-goose))

### goose egg
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; no other citations found on PP or google - plenty of similies, none of this metaphorical use.]

>  In most localities the basal bed is a quartz conglomerate containing well-rounded pebbles up to 4 in. in diameter - the `goose eggs' of the miners. <i>(1939,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 39 (NS): 49</i>) [NOTES: (Williamson-Naseby)]

### goose-beak whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka goose-beaked whale, see beaked whale]

>  Dr. Hector has just received from Mr. E. M. Edgcumbe a photogram of the supposed moa's head, 3ft. in length, lately found near Tauranga. It proves to be the skull of the goose-beak whale (<i>Epiodon</i>) <i>(Oct 23, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Finding that "Pelorus Jack" has been wrongly classified in the genus beluga as a "white whale," he has transferred the creature to the genus Ziphius Cavirostris, believing him to be a "goose-beak whale." <i>(Sep 16, 1904,Feilding Star,3</i>)

>  The skull of a goose-beak whale was unearthed during drainage operations near the Forbury racecourse at Dunedin. <i>(Mar 3, 1914,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

>  The goose-beak whale (<i> Ziphius cavirostris </i>) has a short beak with the lower jaw slightly longer than the upper and turned up a little at the tip. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/136</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  It was identified by museum authorities... as a goose-beaked whale. <i>(1944,NZEF Times,III/135</i>)

### goose-neck binder
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs. farming)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The Woods was probably the first string binder tried here. [in 1880s]. It was known as the `goose-neck' binder because it had a very long circular arm resembling the neck of a goose, which threw the sheaves away so effectively that in some cases when opening up a paddock, they went right over the fence. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,169,1954 ed.</i>)

### goose-saw
 
 <i>n.</i> A saw which cuts cross-grain; cf pendulum saw.

>  [North Auckland Timber-Workers] Men in charge of main goose-saws, 7s... saw-doctor, GBP3 per week. <i>(1901,Awards etc.,III. 8</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> They went past the goose and circular saws and the moving platform of the of the breakdowns to the skids ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,197,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1950,Landfall,14-125</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton)]

>  GOOSE-SAW A pendulum docking saw. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 1957,NZ Timber Journal,49/1</i>)

>  <b>GROVER</b> He was a slabby, loading slabs of useless wood onto a trolley and carting them away. Eighteen months later he graduated to the goose saw. About two feet across, it is used for docking the ends of sawn timber, and is fixed to the end of a beam that swings like a pendulum from a rafter. <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,34</i>)

### gooseberry
 
 <i>n.</i> [WW1, France]

>  <b>STEWART</b> .. the slight losses sustained in the bombardment, and the energy with which the gaps in the wire were at once filled by ready-made circles of loose tangled wire known as `gooseberries', all reflected the highest credit on the soldierly qualities of the defence. <i>(1921,NZ Division,147</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ? =poroporo.

>  <b>LUSH</b> I have had promised me several plants of the Native gooseberry, the fruit with a smooth skin like a cherry and about that size, but a bright yellow when ripe. ... it bears two crops in the year, one in June - the other in March. <i>(Feb 3, 1852,Auckland Journals,102,1971 ed.</i>)

### goosed
 
 <i>adj.</i> ruined, done for (? from cook one's goose).

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> He borrows my horse ... because, as he explains, I probably lost him twenty pounds when I let old Ginger clear out - everybody knows that a horse is goosed after it has cleared out once - ... <i>(1924,Me and Gus,20</i>) [NOTES: 1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm]

>  <b>FENWICK</b> He's quite a stranger in the roost / Who says-he's `stonkered' `sunk' or `goosed'. <i>(1949,Rhyme or Reason</i>) [NOTES: `I've had it']

### goosefoot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FAT HEN (<i>Chenopodium album</i>), called also white fat hen or goose foot [1967 goosefoot], is found in gardens and cultivated fields in great abundance almost everywhere, but [sic] most aggressive in the drier agricultural districts. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,67,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>goosefoot [=] crowfoot grass: fathen [s]</i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,30</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>clammy goosefoot [s] Chenopodium pumilio</i> R.Br. <i>Ibid.</i>30<i> goosefoot, clammy [s] Chenopodium pumilio</i> R.Br. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,14</i>)

>  CLAMMY GOOSEFOOT (<i>Chenopodium pumilo</i>). ... This Australian weed is fairly common in the north of the South Island and many parts of the North Island in open but not dense pasture, ... <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,29</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>goosefoot, glaucous [s] Chenopodium ambiguum</i> R.Br. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,30</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> red fat hen.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> RED FAT HEN (<i>Chenopodium murale</i>), also called nettle-leaved fat hen or roadsides and waste places throughout both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,68,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Chenopodium glaucum</i> var. <i>ambiguum </i> <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Chenopodium glaucum</i> var. <i>ambiguum </i> oak-leaved goosefoot <i>(1911,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on the Dune-areas of NZ]

### gooseman
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BENNETT</b> [Timber-trade terms include] ... goose man (`drag' and `goose' are various types of saw). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  GOOSEMAN The operator of a goose-saw. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 1957,NZ Timber Journal,49/1</i>)

### gooseneck
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> GOOSENECK: Strong iron fitted on pole connecting hind wheels to back. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]

### goosey
 
 <i>n.</i> gooseman [forestry]

>  <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### goovy
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori carrying girdle

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [reference to shifting house on Wellington Coast 1850s] I do not [34] know how many real carriers we had, but altogether they [the Maoris] looked quite a large company each man ready to put his `goovy' (Maori carrying girdle) as we called it, round the load appointed, and get it on to his shoulders. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,33</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 33-34]

### gopher bird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There was a rash of `gopher-birds' that erupted all over our railway stations in 1946, the questions of Chad, the trail of Kilroy: all these fictions came from communities of men suddenly thrown together ... Chad from the R.A.F., Kilroy from the U.S. Army, the `gopher-bird' from our army. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23:211</i>) [NOTES: Pearson `Fretful Sleepers']

### Gordon and Gotch
 
 <i>n.</i> a watch [ORIGIN: rhyming slang, prison slang]

>  Then, on the way to the shovel, I'm so elephant's trunk, I drop me Gordon and Gotch in the ali baba. Gordon and Gotch....watch <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### Gordon Hutter
 
 <i>n.</i> butter [(obsolesc)rhyming slang ca1949 mainly Auckland eg Pass the Gordon Hutter]

### Gordzone
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Godzone](go-gra.md#godzone))

### gorge
 
 <i>adj.</i> gorgeous [1984 16 M E81 Pakuranga Coll. 28 GORG [GORGEOUS] -->1984 17 M E107Pakuranga Coll. 22B GORGE -->1984 15 F E1 Pakuranga Coll. 4 GORGEOUS -->1984 15 F E1 Pakuranga Coll. 28 GORGEOUS -->1984 14 F E119Wgton Girls C. 28 GORGEOUS -->1984 16 F E29 Pakuranga Coll. 4 GORGEOUS (GORGEARS) -->1984 14 F E121Wgton Girls C. 4 GORGOUS]



 
 <i>n.</i> Ravine, canyon rarely used.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> This distance remains ... so far south as the `Gorge', where the Manawatu river bursts its way through the range; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 240 A series of narrow gorges, covered with flax, fern, and grass; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,88</i>)

>  [check quot] <i>(1851,Otago Journal,125</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ... but as we looked up the river-bed, we saw two large and gloomy gorges, at the end of each of which were huge glaciers, ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vi/82</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Continued on our course up the river - a very bushy sideling of a steep mountain gorge, with the white foam of the river some hundreds of feet below us - . <i>(Apr 30, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 30 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 409 ..]

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the picturesque tents in the deep mountain gorge ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,17,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... the hill-spurs get steeper, and the gorges get deeper, ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,184</i>)

>  <b>EDGE-PARTINGTON</b> The word `gorge' hardly defines it. It is really a valley with most precipitous sides .... The road is what is called a bluff road, that is, it is cut out of the side of the precipice. <i>(1883,Random Rot,325</i>)

>  Gorge.-A correspondent has been good enough to remind me of a usage of this word which I believe is peculiar to Canterbury: i.e., any main valley above the (technically correct) g. of a river. For instance, musterers always speak of Hakatere, Mespotamia, Manuka Point, Lake Heron, etc., etc., as being in the Ashburton, Rakaia or Rangitata G. This usage goes back to at least 1894. As my correspondent says, `misty gorges are particularly prevalent in the MacKenzie Country'; so I suppose the usage will become poetical. [Also in 1951 Acland Early Canterbury Runs Glossary 369] <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  Gorge: Used to describe a narrow river channel and also a whole river catchment. e.g. Rakaia Gorge. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Gorge: A term common in Canterbury. Refers to the areas of hill country in the upper reaches of the main rivers, i.e. the Rakaia Gorge is the area of country from where the river disappears into the hills and up to its headworks. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,187</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Then what is called the "gorge" portion of the valley is entered. <i>(Sep 24, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,29</i>)

### gorge block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm or station area of steep land bordering a gorge.

>  <b>EVANS</b> In 1893 Evans purchased the gorge block from Sara Elworthy. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,209</i>)

### gorge country
 
 <i>n.</i> steep land that faces each side of the river gorges (principally in Canterbury and Otago).

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But that gorge country has a story of sweat and tears which I thought should be recorded. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,15</i>)

### gorge run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country grazing property aligning one of the river gorges in the South Island.

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Runholders say that in sharing notions of 'front' and 'back', the 'gorge runs are similar from one end of the South Island to the other' <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,138</i>)

### gorge station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country grazing property aligning one of the river gorges in the South Island.

>  The property ranges from 1700 up to 4600 feet at the back, and like most gorge stations, we can get snow at any time during winter and spring. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Spring 23</i>)

>  Wanted shepherd for high country gorge station ... <i>(Aug 23, 2002,Dominion,C3</i>)

### gorge-bound
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>PASCOE</b> ... the major obstacles lay in gorge-bound beds of the rivers and in the swiftness of the rivers themselves. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,10</i>)

### gorge-musterer
 
 <i>n.</i> [Check 1951 Acland Early Canterbury Runs]

### gorger
 
 <i>n.</i> [circus argot]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> Archie gave a grunt, and away he walks to the gorger, and asked him to invite the stranger in. [fn] [circus] Proprietor <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,3</i>)

### gorgey
 
 <i>adj.</i> having gorges

>  Is it not steep and gorgey in a great many places? <i>(1896,AJHR,D.-4/146</i>)

### gorgy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) gorgeous

>  Gorgy evening bags: <i>Hype</i>-scouts-about-town always need an assortment of fabulous evening bags, especially at this time of year. <i>(Dec 3, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> having gorges (See [also gorgey](go-gra.md#gorgey))

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Some very ugley [sic] gorgy gaps are looming up at the head of the flat. <i>Ibid.</i> 205 But I could not get down ..., the creek being gorgy with a succession of waterfalls. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,132</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Jan 29 C. Douglas Report to G. Roberts in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 132]

>  But largely owing to the difficulty of transporting stores up this extremely gorgy stream, the prospectors did not penetrate more than five or six miles. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (N.S.): 24</i>) [NOTES: Morgan-Mikomu]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Opossums inhabited the bush in the gorgy part of the valley in fair numbers, and had been trapped for several seasons, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 137 We had climbed some hundred feet since leaving the creek bed and the going had become very gorgy and rough. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,73</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> It's a narrow gorgy valley that only gets narrower and gorgier. <i>(1989,Bullock Creek,42</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> Between five and nine miles the river valley is more confined and gorgy, and for about two miles is difficult to pass even on foot. <i>(1895,Report on Geol. of SW Nelson,81</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The innocent stream as seen from the light aircraft proved to be a bit gorgy with a waterfall leaping down. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,163</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of ravines or rivers] steep-sided

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In places it became narrow and gorgy ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,134</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> We soon found ourselves in a 'gorgy' creek. We could see three kilometres of 'gorgy' river which took a bend to the left just after the County stream came in. [gorgey] <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,83</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We had climbed some hundred feet since leaving the creek bed and the going had become very gorgy and rough. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,137</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> .. the weather let us down into a tributary of the Wanganui River bent with gorgy elbows and riddled with cascades. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,182</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> It took three quarters of an hour to drag that pig around the side of that gorgey creek ... <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,143</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> We came down a tight gorgy little creek ... <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,73</i>)

### gorie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our first contract was the same old gorie where we won our fame - viz., swinging the old banjo. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 33</i>)

### gorilla
 
 <i>n.</i> A guerilla (? bush ranger) in the Land Wars. [hist. nonce humorous]

>  The `Gorillas' as they [Volunteer Rifles - Taranaki] are popularly called. <i>Ibid.</i> July 3 2/53 (A.S. Atkinson) Col. Warre will allow two gorilla parties instead of one - 45 each - volunteers I mean and [ampersand] picked men. <i>(1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/591,1960 ed.</i>)

### gorm
 
 <i>n.</i> A brainless person; a fool. [(schoolboys') [Omit: from gormless, from Sc. dial?] 1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 1 GORM (2) -->ca1951 Wellington College -->ca1939 Nelson College `gormo' -->1984 14 F E126Wgton Girls C. 1 GORM -->1984 14 F E135Wgton Girls C. 1 GORM]

 <i>adj.</i> [1953 14 M A19 Thames DHS 1 GORMEY]

### Goron
 
 <i>n.</i> a hoon from Gore

>  Police in Queenstown refer to the Gore hoons as "Gorons". An entry in the police occurrence book in Queenstown for September 30 reads: "Gorons just can't resist the bright lights of Queenstown and the comfy beds of the police cells." <i>(Nov 10, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

### Gorsave
 
 <i>adj.</i> [?? from 'God save (the King)']

>  The great day was at hand when the frenzied fatuity of the Gorsave type of patriot descended to the very lowest depths of imbecility. <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205/1/1</i>)

### gorse
 
 <i>n.</i> A wild, prickly, evergreen European shrub with yellow flowers, which grows as a weed in New Zealand and Australia. -->Also called furze. [1953 Feb R.Mason `Westport district heard twice from local people.']

>  In case any dividing fence shall be destroyed or injured by reason of the falling of timber ... or the firing of scrub fern toitoi gorse or flax ... <i>(1973,Acts of Wellington Legislature,63 8.</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Fencing Act in 1873 Acts of Wellington Legislature 63 8.]

>  ... whereon there shall be any gorse or furze ... <i>(1873,Acts of Wellington Legislature,90 31.</i>) [NOTES: 1871 Highways Act in 1873 90 31.]

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: gorse <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> have gorse in one's pocket; to be tight-fisted, not to put one's hand in one's pocket. [1985 Wellington used of a tight-fisted husband]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> untidy, bedraggled [Common from at least 1940s on: He looks as though he's been dragged through a gorse bush.]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>dwarf gorse [s] Ulex minor</i> Roth <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,23</i>)

### gorse bush
 
 <i>v.</i> dragged through a gorse bush backwards - bedraggled

### gorse grubbing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the chipping of gorse, a noxious weed in New Zealand.

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Contractors did all the multifarious jobs of Southern estates - chaff cutting, mangold hoeing, gorse grubbing. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,121</i>)

### gorse hedge
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED has only cit. for 1888]

>  I stop by a yellow gorse hedge and listen to the thrushes. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### gorse knife
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Randal kicked aside his gorse-knife - he had been cutting brush in the gully beyond. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,80</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> `Give me two bob for it,' pleads the auctioneer. `Well then, chuck in the saw and those two gorse knives as well. Now then a quid for the lot?' <i>(1962,Country Occasions,132</i>)

### gorse over
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  A Sturmer Pippin and an Irish Peach on the property came into bloom simultaneously, and Grandfather Sisson `gorsed over' a spur of each, pollenising one with the other and then gorsing them back again. [- on origin of Lord Wolseley apple in article on Sisson family's orchard.] <i>(1953,Orchardist NZ,26(4)/5</i>)

### gorse pocket/s
 
 <i>n.</i> (New Zealand (Southern South Island)) said of a person who is miserly; a miserly act [used in phrases, particularly in the South Island.]

>  She called me a "middle-class patroniser of the underclass" and told me to grow some "gorse pockets". <i>(Feb 23, 2015,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  I've always had gorse pockets. We've migrated into certain jobs among ourselves and I'm certainly the money controller. <i>(Jan 23, 2010,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  Yes, you heard it right - the Labour leader and union boss is actually 'Mr Gorse Pockets' rather than a champion of workers' rights. <i>(Feb 18, 2015,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.3news.co.nz/nznews/opinion-littles-no-pay-shocker]

>  Many cultures celebrate the tea break, though corporate culture may not be among them. To those "gorse pockets" I say: take a tea break and have a good hard look at yourselves. <i>(Mar 28, 2015,Press, The,4</i>)

>  When it comes to Christmas shopping, Kiwis have gorse in their pockets compared with shoppers from other countries. <i>(Dec 21, 2004,Press, The,B5</i>)

>  Nordmeyer is referred to as "an enemy of fun" and "old gorse-pockets Nordmeyer." <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: undercoatblog.blogspot.com/2010-10-01.archive]

### gorse-cutter
 
 <i>n.</i> a machine for cutting gorse

>  I bought a Hedgehog gorse-cutter, attached it to a Fordson tractor and went to work. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/26</i>)

### gorse-cutting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... he showed his entrepreneurial flair at Wrights Bush by starting a sideline in gorse-cutting. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/26</i>)

### gorse-grubber
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm worker employed to dig out gorse.

>  For all his small size, Joe was strong and skilful at using a draining spade for grubbing gorse, and in those days I was regarded as the fastest gorse-grubber in the district. <i>(Apr 2, 1956,NZ Dairy Exporter,116</i>)

### gorse-slasher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... were given grubbers and gorse-slashers. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,253</i>)

### gorsepocket
 
 <i>n.</i> A mean person [1987 p.c. D.McGill]

>  <b>McGILL</b> gorsepocket mean person; phr. he's got gorse in his pocket. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,52</i>)

### goshore
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [goashore](go-gra.md#goashore))

### gospel-grinder
 
 <i>n.</i> A derog. term popular in the early 20C. for a street-preacher, evangelist, esp. a member of the Salvation Army. (Perhaps mainly used by Truth, in its fondness for alliterative epithets and dislike of Salvationists. Cf gospel-thumper. [(? obsolete)]

>  (etc) A small push of psalm singers at Christchurch applied to the boss-sweater for a new organ ... greedy, cringing gospel-grinders. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/1/7</i>)

### goulash
 
 <i>n.</i> A rough stew, carelessly concocted. [(Esp. trampers and boarding school.) ->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 22B GOOLASH (2) -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 22B GOULASH -->1953 16 M A30 Thames DHS 22B GOO-LASH -->1953 15 M A24 Thames DHS 22B GOULASH -->1953 15 M A27 Thames DHS 22B GOULASH (GOOLASH) -->1953 15 M A26 Thames DHS 22B GOULASH (GOOLASH)] (See [also goo](go-gra.md#goo))

>  <b>JOHNSTON (ed)</b> ... remember that goulash we used to turn out up at the hut. <i>(1946,21 Years with Boots and Pack,38</i>)

### gourd
 
 <i>n.</i> The native Pacific gourd; see also calabash. [178? Hawkesworth Voyages (Cook etc) (And). <i>Lagenaria vulgaris </i>]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Gourds (<i>Lagenaria vulgaris </i>) were brought from Hawaiki. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/156</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Hue ...gourd; curcurbita sp. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,37</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hue ... 1. Lagenaria vulgaris, calabash-gourd. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,78</i>)

>  <i>Lagenaria vulgaris </i> <i>(1901,TrNZI,33:</i>)

>  <i>Lagenaria vulgaris </i> <i>(1903,TrNZI,35</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> (And)<i>Lagenaria vulgaris </i> <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Lagenaria</i>

>  <b>GRACE</b> Their hospitality was carried out in a very respectful manner, and the poor woman wished to do me still more honour. She took some preserved meat out of a `taha' (Native gourd). <i>(Oct 28, 1871,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1871 Oct 28 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 237]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Sicyos australis</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Sicyos australis</i> Australian gourd <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [kuri](kiw-kwi.md#kuri))

 <i>n.</i>

### Gov-Gen
 
 <i>n.</i> the Governor-General; cf. G.G. [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  `Yuk Yuk', of Wellington, suggests the story of Gov.-Gen's dogs would have made a good start. <i>(Oct 19, 1972,Dominion,4/10</i>)

### Governess
 
 <i>n.</i> A humorous solecism for the wife of Governor (-General). [ca1930's West Coast folk tale of a visit by G-G. `We welcome you, Lord Bledisloe, and especially your charming Governess.']

>  Mrs Gore-Brown (the Governess as James called her) <i>(1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/541</i>)

### Government
 
 <i>n.</i> Used absolutely without the def. art. esp. in official correspondence.

>  <b>SUGDEN</b> A party about emigrating to New Zealand begs most respectfully to solicit the assistance of Government in their undertaking. ... One thing I must beg Your Lordship's most serious attention to - that is, the heavy duty (1/- per foot) on timber imported into N.S. Wales from Zealand <i>(Jan 18, 1821,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1821 Jan 18 Robert Sugden to Earl of Bathurst in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/516]

>  Left Ch.Ch. I had next door to an offer of 300[pounds] p.a. from Government to keep the boundary of Canterbury and Otago but I could not accept ... <i>(Jan 29, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,120,1950 ed.</i>)

### Government agent
 
 <i>n.</i> [see agent]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Mr. Maclean at this time was M.H.R. for Napier, and also Government Agent for the East Coast district. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,91</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> It [movement for repudiation] originated in Maori aspirations to repossess land purchased by government agents in the 1850s and by private individuals under the 10-owner system, 1865-1873. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,12</i>)

### Government block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Government-owned area of farmland under development, administered by the Department of Lands and Survey.

>  <b>PULLEN</b> When we married Bill took a farm managers [sic] job on a Government block in the King Country .. We stayed managing until August 1975, then the block we were on was due to be settled so we had to find other employment. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,5</i>)

### Government Buildings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> There is quite a lot [of land] reclaimed already [in Wellington], and enormous government buildings are in process of erection on part of it. ... They will interfere with the view from Government House, which is only a few hundred yards distant on the landward side of them. <i>(1870,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,(19) 23</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> The Government Building is an immense structure in Italian style; it covers an area of two acres, and is said to be the largest wooden edifice in the world. Than there are Government House, where the colonial governor lives and exercises the duties of ruler of New Zealand; ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,248</i>)

>  Deputation to the Honble the Premier/ ... A deputation of representatives of the Trades and Labor [sic] Unions waited on the Hon the Premier at Government Buildings ... <i>(Sep 1, 1890,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/559,1960 ed.</i>)

### government culler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contracted deer, goat or possum shooter.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> In 1947 the skin price dropped, possibly when war contracts expired, and I sought employment as a Government culler. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,18</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> During one of several trips to Greymouth, three of us met some former Government cullers at the gallops. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,82</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> After spending many years as a government culler .. I found myself becoming wedded to mountain life. <i>(1964,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,9</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> .. we came to the spot where the government cullers had had their camp. <i>(1964,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,45</i>)

### Government cut
 
 <i>n.</i> [(forestry)]

>  GOVERNMENT CUT A length of log, either at the stump or at the head of a tree which is left in the bush as a result of wasteful cutting. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### Government head
 
 <i>n.</i> a standard measure of water supplied to a claim [(mining)] (See [cf. hogburn](../H#hogburn),[sluice head](sho-spa.md#sluice-head))

>  ... a race of .... twenty Government heads of water <i>(1870,AJHR,D/40/7</i>) [NOTES: Reports of Goldfields]

>  [see quotation hogburn head] <i>(1872,AJHR,G/4/29</i>) [NOTES: Ulrich: Report on Goldfields]

### Government House
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [check J.Logan Campbell Poenamo [1842] and Letters to his Father [TS] Auck. Inst and Museum Library]

>  I had just returned to the ship when I observed great confusion in the Bay, ... where the greater part of the Government House and furniture has already been landed. <i>(Oct 3, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct 3 SARAH MATHEW Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 193]

>  I shall describe Govt House. Govt House is a Mauri built dwelling of Wattle and dab and Wattle and dab is this. A number of stakes are .. <i>(Jun 5, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,226,1966 ed.</i>)

>  Government House, Russell, Bay of Islands, 10 November 1840. <i>(1841,GBPP,1841 (No.311) 126</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Nov 10 Hobson to Secretary of State for the Colonies in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 126]

>  TEMPORARY GOVERNMENT HOUSE AND DOMAIN <i>(1842,GBPP,(No.569) [facing] 136</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Aug 12 Map of Auckland in GBPP 1842 (No.569) [facing] 136]

>  Witness our trusty and well-beloved George Grey, Esquire, Governor-in-Chief ... at Government House, Auckland, <i>(1949,Selected Documents,26</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 Proclamation of New Charter of NZ in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 26]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The want of a Government House is a serious drawback. Even beyond the circle of the visiting world, the destruction of the Old House has been, in every respect, a public loss. <i>(1853,Auckland,30</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> `On the high land east of the town [of Auckland] was the long government house in a conspicuous situation; a church of brick in the process of erection; and a solid barrack for the soldiery near it.' ### Government rag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Shinplasters.- The fact that private traders found it necessary themselves, to issue paper notes in denominations below five shillings suggests that at the outset ... the Fitzroy notes were not issued for values below five shillings. The private paper notes were issued for small change, redeemable mostly in `Five Shillings, in Government debentures.' Both the debentures and the private notes were issued on poor paper, and soon became known as `Government Rags' and `Shinplasters' respectively. The term `Shinplaster' for paper money appears to have come to New Zealand from America, via New South Wales. *[fn] In the latter Colony the issue of paper money of a denomination lower than twenty shillings was prohibited in 1826 to promote the circulation of sterling instead of a colonial currency, and the prohibition was welcomed by the people as `preventing the Colony being inundated with beggarly rags' and dollar and half-dollar notes `used often as a paltry contrivance to evade payment of debt,' vide `The Australian,' 19 July 1826. [fn] Various reasons have been given for the origin of this curious term for paper money issued in the forties. The most likely explanation comes from America (p. 650 The Numismatist, 1938), where it is stated that in the good old days post-boys and out-riders, wearing high boots, were required, at times, to pay toll-fees at toll-gates, and in order to facilitate progress they placed paper money or coins in bags in their boot-tops, against their shins - hence the term `shin-plaster.' <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,158</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> The [Governor Fitzroy's (1844)] Debentures, popularly known as `government rags', were given various nicknames such as assignats, `shinplasters', and `flash notes'. All these terms had reference to worthless paper money, some of which had been issued in the past. Assignats was the name given to large amounts of paper money issued in France during the French Revolution, but which depreciated to such as extent to be almost worthless. `Shinplasters'--sometimes written `skinplasters'--had its origin in North America, and again referred to depreciated paper money, while `flash notes' was a popular term in use for any counterfeit money or worthless forged paper money. <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,40</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

[Obs.] "A few prisoners, or government men, as the convict servants are uniformly designated by courtesy." J. D. ### Government valuation
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a property valuation by, originally, the Government Valuation Department

>  'What's the GV?' is the first question most buyers ask when they're in the market for a new house. Actually, as of 1998 there is no such thing. The job of valuing properties moved out of the government arena three years ago. The valuation of properties became contestable, and the former Government Valuation Department became a business called Quotable Value New Zealand (QV). However, councils right around the country are still required by law to put what is now called a Rateable Value on properties, and in Nelson city the tender for this work was won by QV. It is to be emphasized that the valuations are carried out totally independently of the council, with the property owner having the right to dispute the valuation with the valuer before they are set. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Nelson Live</i>) [NOTES: Taken from Nelson City Council website: www.nelsoncitycouncil.co.nz]

>  Government valuations, often used as a selling tool, did not necessarily reflect current market value. A home in Breaker Bay which recently sold for $235,000 above its GV was an example of that. <i>(Jul 3, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  <b>McMURRAN</b> `Work' is `graft'; salary, or wage is `screw'; `rubbers' are named `goloshes'; a silk-hat is a `bell-topper'. `Lollies' is the colonial term for confectionery etc ... `Coo-ee' (prolonged) is used as a long-distance call, instead of `hello'. In ringing up a telephone, instead of `Hello, central,' people here ask, `Are you there' or `Is that exchange' - Then give the number required as in London `shouting means treating'. Being `had' is a term used here when a person feels he has been imposed upon or cheated. <i>(1904,From New York to NZ,144</i>)
