>  (And.) <i>Pseudopanax crassifolium</i> grasss-tree <i>(Jul 29, 1919,Vernacular Names</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> He cut a grass tree with his pocket knife, took a rope from his saddle, fixed a loop on the end of the pole, ... <i>(1970,Tat,74</i>) [NOTES: [Otago] [?lancewood]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Pseudopanax crassifolius</i> LANCEWOOD ... Horoeka ... Grass tree (local name for juveniles) <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,82</i>)

>  <i>Pseudopanax crassifolium</i> grass tree <i>(1877,TrNZI,9:</i>)

### grass tree gum
 
 <i>n.</i> [[JD notes: Curious. Seems to refer to cabbage tree which doesn't exude gum. Only tree that really does is kauri. HWO: could this be a confusion of the neinei `grass-tree' growing on kauri, with kauri gum also found in crotches of kauri trees and phs associated with neinei.??]]

>  Some friends ... have written us to send them from New Zealand two or three tons of `Grass Tree Gum' (or Gum <i>Acroides</i>). This gum ... is said to grow in quantities in New Zealand. ... The Grass Tree, we believe, chiefly grows in low swampy places; and may be known by its peculiar stumpy stem covered with a kind of grass. When cut ... the gum exudes plentifully; and ... it has an aromatic quality. ... we may - provided a regular supply can be insured - soon see New Zealand Grass Tree Gum commanding a good quotation in the London Price Lists. <i>(Aug 12, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

### grass tree swamp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... we had a great deal of wading up to our knees, besides crossing many narrow grass-tree swamps, in which an injudicious step often plunged us to a much greater depth. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 245 [JD prob. <i>Cordyline</i>]]

### grass-bound
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> (get quot) <i>(1921,Tutira,197</i>)

### grass-money
 
 <i>n.</i> ? money received for the grazing of animals on common land of parish.

>  [Maoris get] `utu-karahi' or grass-money for the depasturage of that which is self sown and unused by themselves. [frequent use in this context] <i>(1862,AJHR,E5A-12</i>) [NOTES: (Turton, Canterbury)]

### grass-paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area newly made into pasture.

>  .. only one attempt at the electrified wire there suffices to remind them [the sheep] that a grass paddock underfoot is worth several on the other side of a fence which apparently hits back. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20</i>)

>  When the weather is suitable run a chain or grass harrow over the grass-paddocks to scatter the stock-droppings. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,220</i>)

### grass-seed gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of harvesters whose task was to cut and bag cocksfoot grass-seed [1860s-1950s.]

>  <b>COULSON</b> Farmers, farm-hands, grass-seed gangs and every available person in Pigeon Bay turned out with wet sacks and carried water up from the creeks in the valleys, and within twenty-four hours the fire was under control. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,70</i>)

>  <b>COULSON</b> The previous January, 1897, would have seen a fire of equal proportions, had it not been for the tremendous efforts of farmers and grass-seed gangs, and a more favourable wind factor. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,69</i>)

### grass-seed holiday
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the leave from school granted to children to help with the cocksfoot harvest.

>  The children went back to school after New Year till the cocksfoot seed was ripe, then were allowed holidays (about a fortnight..) to help to harvest it. The children just loved these 'grass-seed holidays' as they were called. <i>(Mar 24, 1969,Weekly News,45</i>)

### grass-seeder
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sower of seed in bush-burn areas.

>  <b>CARR</b> Underscrubbers vary from 30s a week .. Grass seeders 1s to 1s 6d an hour and tucker themselves. <i>(1913,Country Work,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cocksfooter, a collector of grass-seed for sale OEDS grass 13. - seeder A person who gathers grass-seed for sale; also attrib; hence grass-seeding vbl.sb. [(Banks Peninsula)]

>  ... that the industry will not give employment to half the usual number of men, and that a dead loss will be sustained by grass-seeders and croppers alike; ... <i>(Dec 4, 1910,NZ Truth,287/8/3</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> The grass-seeder would entrust his cheque to the squatter <i>(1936,Poor Scholar,202</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,211</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The official issue of clothing [at Roto Roa - `the Island' centre for alcoholics] is a wide-brimmed `grass-seeder' straw hat. <i>(1922,Happy Wanderer,211</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cocksfoot cutter or harvester [1860-1950] or farmer who produces grass for seed.

>  I am sitting in a tent - one of the many that go to make up a grass-seeders' camp .. a few remarks from a working grass-seeder upon the subject of grass-seeding may be interesting to some. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,338</i>)

>  <b>COULSON</b> A cloud burst on that day not only brought ruin to ripened crops, but loss of life to two grass-seeders. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,72</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> The grass-seeder would entrust his cheque to the squatter. <i>(1936,Poor Scholar,202</i>)

>  <b>COULSON</b> One can imagine the rafters of the tiny building ringing as they sang 'The Grass-Seeders' Song' written by their teacher.' <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> One who sows grass-seed on cleared burns.

>  <b>CARR</b> Underscrubbers vary from 30s a week ... Grass seeders 1s to 1s 6d an hour and tucker themselves. <i>(1913,Country Work,31</i>)

### grass-seeding
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sowing of seed in bush-burn areas.

>  <b>MARTIN</b> .. he took up bushfelling, shearing, grass-seeding and made good money. [grass seeding] <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cocksfooting [(Banks Peninsula)]

>  ... when the [Lyttelton] men ... see the chance of making a few extra quid by crossing the harbour and indulging in a few weeks' Grass-seeding <i>(Oct 10, 1905,NZ Truth,17/1/8</i>)

>  The prolonged drought in Canterbury has ruined the cocksfoot crop on Banks Peninsula, and grass-seeding this year, which has annually furnished big cheques for Bill and Jim, will be an attenuated industry, and a delusion, and a snare. <i>(Dec 24, 1910,NZ Truth,287/8/3</i>)

>  (Ngata) ... the contribution of the Maori people to the economic progress of N.Z. - ... Dairying in Taranaki, at Nuhaka, Ruatoki and in the far North, railway and road construction, ... on a smaller scale carpentering, motor-driving, threshing, grass-seeding, droving, shepherding, domestic service, teaching, clerical & other work in the Public Service, store-keeping, laundry-work &c &c. <i>(1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/95,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> ... grass-seeding is conducive to a princely thirst. <i>Ibid.</i> 204 Grass-seeding is rather characteristic of these parts [Banks Peninsula]. <i>(1936,Poor Scholar,202</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the growing and harvesting of grass for seed.

>  Grass seeding started in January and a man could make ten or twelve pounds for the season, which was quite a sum of money. Most marriages were arranged for after grass seeding. <i>(1945,BLANK ENTRY,81</i>) [NOTES: in KEEGAN (1983) The First Generation]

>  .. a few remarks from a working grass-seeder upon the subject of grass-seeding may be interesting to some. Grass-seeding, then - the kind that I speak of - is the name usually given to cocksfoot harvesting. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,338</i>)

>  This is the weather for grass seeding, and reports from many localities indicate that the grass this year has a good bottom. <i>(Jan 16, 1941,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  <b>COMBS</b> Enthusiastic boys made a wonderful difference during hay-making and grass-seeding. Grass-seeding was a different proposition. <i>(1951,Growing Up in the Forty-Mile Bush,98</i>)

>  <b>COULSON</b> So severely hit by the misfortunes of the season were some of the growers that they decided to leave grass-seeding for good. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,83</i>)

### grasshopper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Grasshopper .. .. kowhitiwhiti, mawhitiwhiti .. .. Phaulacridium marginale <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> informer [(prison slang)]

### Grasshoppers
 
 <i>n.</i> [hist.]

McCosh Clark, No. 6 company...; this company [?the composite force, ? or No. 6], some time afterwards, had a uniform of green cloth, and were dubbed the `Grasshoppers'. <i>(1879,Waikato War,52</i>)

 <i>n.</i> making love on the grass [WW2) ca 1940 2NZEF (Len Climo)]

### grassing down
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sowing of pasture or lawn grass.

>  Grassing down in the orchard..The main disadvantage of grassing down is competition for water and nutrients by the sward. Citrus growers should be cautious about grassing down their orchards. <i>(Jul 15, 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,81</i>)

### grassland
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in the names of plants: (See [buttercup](bri-byo.md#buttercup),[daisy](d-dif.md#daisy),[orchid](on-ore.md#orchid))

### grassless
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of weather] not conducive to pasture growth.

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> A farmer had one acre of mangels feeding 200 ewes, seven cows, and ten pigs for eleven weeks of winter, grassless weather. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,96</i>)

### grassline
 
 <i>n.</i> The line or level on a mountain above which no grass grows. 1892 Exp. Douglas 176 1959 Tararua xiii 45. [(trampers')]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> It is very common all along the ranges close to the grass line and might be introduced as an ornamental tree in gardens or parks ... <i>Ibid.</i>237 Generally called the mountain duck, as it is mostly found in the rocky torrents up to the grass line. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,176,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1892 June 8 C. Douglas, Report to J. Strauchon in Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas (1957) 176]

>  <b>HARPER</b> On Douglas's return we began to `blaze' our track to the grass line behind Camp 2. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,64</i>)

>  The subalpine scrub occurs .... forming a distinct belt & barrier between the forest and the grassline ... <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/400</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  Behind the bush-edge fringe of mountain beech, silver-beech extended some hundreds of feet up the mountainside, then mountain came in again to the grassline. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,48</i>)

### grassroots
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; in the term 'at the grass roots'; also Aust]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> At the grassroots: Gold on the surface of the ground. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Mining Terms]

>  It is now some years ago since a farmer on the Terrace picked up a nugget weighing 25dwts, and though the find was much discussed no prospecting was attempted until about twelve months ago, when Mr Matheson, also a farmer on the Crown Terrace, set in to work some ground where the gold could be found at the grass roots. <i>(Oct 7, 1891,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

### gratings
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp.use] slat-floors of woolshed sheep pens.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For the purpose of sluicing down the gratings themselves there are also two sixty-foot-high pressure hoses. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,76</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Each morning the rouseabout, reasonably, got the dead ones off the gratings and threw them out the window of the shed. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,187</i>)

### graunch
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. (the result of) a grinding collision or graze -->ca1945 at least -->2. (trampers') =grind n., a stiff pull up a hill: ca1951 the last final graunch up hill (NCC Small)



 
 <i>v.</i> to smash glass or china [ca1950: used by D.E. Hurley. ca1951 B.F. Kay Don't graunch my new coat. -->ca1951 Elvidge graunched a pass.]



 
 <i>v.</i> (in various senses or applications of grind) [a. ca1951 NCC Small to graunch out a rough piece of work. (`grind out') -->b. ca1951 to graunch a woman (`fuck; grind') --]



 
 <i>v.</i> to gut a deer [1980 Goldie Brown Letter (Check this, he says)]



 
 <i>v.</i> to make a grinding or scraping sound; to grate; to crunch. ca1946 Heard after the Tamahine ran aground: `The ship graunched over the rock bottom - the noise was terrific.'

>  On Sundays, he leaves the dogs at the hut, graunches into the city in a clapped out Morris Oxford smelling of oily rags and sheep dags.. and takes his wife and his eldest brother down to his jazz club by the Avon. <i>(1973,Islands,6/360</i>)

>  That graunching noise emanating from the old parliament buildings last week was a wheel falling off the National Party. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  The plates were damaged when the 4,300 ton ship graunched its forward port side into the old Picton Ferry wharf. <i>(Aug 13, 1962,Dominion,15</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> The van was graunching its way deafeningly up a gentle slope in second gear. <i>(1962,One of Us,167</i>)

>  <b>SIMONS</b> As I close the carriage door, the train graunches, stutters and moves forward. <i>(1979,Harper's Mother,170</i>)

>  graunch v. NZ make a crunching or grinding sound; damage or break (machinery etc.) [Eng. dial.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,328</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> graunch to misengage gears, producing graunching sound; any sound indicative of mishandling or likely to ruin machinery. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to damage, ruin (usually something mechanical). [-->ca1949 (R. Mason) Tramping: to wreck, ruin, spoil, damage (always in a physical sen-->ca1950 (Wellington) of a watch filled with sand.]

### graunched
 
 <i>adj.</i> `ruined' [ca1951 (popular use)]

### graunching
 
 <i>n.</i> a scraping or grinding noise [ca1945 Havelock esp. of launches or dinghies striking gravel.]

>  <b>McGill</b> The door slammed and with some unpleasant metal graunching the gear was engaged and the bus rattled and shuddered off towards Tauranga. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,184</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> Salutes got thrown to all and sundry, she pushed the right knob and the car started and with a lot of graunching as she got it into gear. <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,84</i>)

### graunchingly
 
 <i>adv.</i> horrifyingly; excruciatingly

>  Thus we find ourselves in a graunchingly familiar hall equipped with a squalid kitchen and an excruciatingly painted backdrop of Mount Egmont. <i>(Oct 1, 1979,Dominion,4/1</i>) [NOTES: [From a review by Bruce Mason]]

### gravedigger
 
 <i>n.</i> the old-type steel gramophone needle [ca1951 Wellington Public Library (records there usually played with cane needles)]

### gravel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> I came a cropper at the bottom of the hill. On the loose gravel. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,118</i>)

### gravel pit
 
 <i>n.</i> [[? a North Island use]]

>  <b>PHILLIPS ROCKWOOD</b> - John taking top spit off gravel-pit - ... <i>(Jan 19, 1857,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,65</i>)

>  The remains of a large moa have been brought to light from the old estuary gravel-pits of St. John's Hill ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/285</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> In fact, bush-whacking was the refuge of one type of destitute who could hardly ... have been induced to take work in shop, gravel-pit, railway or any of the recognized avenues of trade or labour, ... <i>(1955,Land of My Children,7</i>)

>  Beside the school ... there is a gravel pit grown over with grass. Boulders lie about, half-hidden by hip-high weeds ... It's like a tiny amphitheatre now. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,36</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> Gravel pits became even more important to the council's work and economy. <i>(1971,Centenary of Invercargill,63</i>)

>  the Gravel Pit: (I can't remember what this is) <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> `I saw many fights in the gravel pit amongst the boys, and on one occasion my sister scratched [78] the face of one boy who was apparently winning in a fight with my brother. Sometimes girls fought in their own way - hair pulling, etc.' (1890; Moeraki)... In towns the same sort of group fighting was usually known as gang-fighting. Different gangs took different sides in the Paper Chase. (1900; Riwaka) Each gang had a corner of the playground. Captured prisoners were held by force and by force alone. There were torn shirts and fingers bent back. When enough prisoners were caught, they would break out by force. (1915; Nelson) Some of these gangs (and the girls' groups or sets too) their secret language and/or codes. ... <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,77</i>)

>  Councillor Rex Nicholls is right to complain about thecity's messy building sites. For the past 10 years the more the new buildings have gone up, the greater the shambles it has looked. Look at The Terrace. It is like one big gravel pit. <i>(Mar 7, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>) [NOTES: [Ordinary Bloke's column]]

### gravel rash
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> The rifle banged. Captain Smythe, his face beautifully patterned with gravel-rash, turned again and leapt at Starkie. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,121</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> The rocking-chair was ... the comforter in all her troubles: a scolding, ... a `gravel-rash' knee, ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,51</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> What I did have was a beautiful gravel rash all down one side of my face and as I ran along the road, ... <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,166</i>)

### gravel-scraper
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dog trials and mustering] a heading dog that is all noise and no effect.

>  Gravel scraper - a heading dog whose runout style shows more clawing and scratching than purposefully gripping the hill and heading with real intent. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  Ineffective dog: a gravel-scraper. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  .. the sheep-worrier (wool classer) and the gravel-scraper. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### gravel-scratcher
 
 <i>n.</i> an infantryman [WWI]

>  <b>WEBB</b> Well, it was no good thinking of it [sc. buying a cavalry horse]; I should have to be a `gravel-scratcher' after all, ... <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,103</i>)

### graveyard
 
 <i>n.</i> [(sailing scows)]

>  <b>EADDY</b> She had gone ashore on what was known as the `graveyard', on the northern side of the entrance to the Kaipara river, and here she had piled up. <i>(1933,Hull Down,158</i>)

### gravy-eye
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) mattery eye, a bacterial disease of potatoes. (See [mattery eye](mar-moa.md#mattery-eye))

>  In that district [Pukekohe] great damage has recently been occasioned by a bacterial disease popularly known as 'gravy-eye' or 'mattery-eye'... <i>(May 21, 1917,NZ Journal of Agriculture,357</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(sailing)]

### gravyboat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Not all inspectors were sombre old gravyboats. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,106</i>)

### Gray Shadow
 
 <i>n.</i> a name for Pelorus Jack

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Travelling from Wellington on the little coaster ... they made a good trip through the ... Pelorus Sound, where sported the playful gray fish `Pelorus Jack' (science often disputed whether he was a porpoise or his exact species). ... [155] For years this famous fish continued ..., and was the only fish in the world protected by Act of Parliament. Eventually the famous `Gray Shadow' disappeared, and the Dominion mourned his loss. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,155</i>)

### Gray's shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <i>Stewart Island Shag Leucocarbo chalconotus </i>(GRAY, 1845) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Bronze shag, Gray's shag. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,119</i>)

### grayling
 
 <i>n.</i> freshwater herring, upokororo

>  The rivers, too, teemed with greyling and whitebait. <i>(1959,Women of Westland,84</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition. See also "NZ grayling"]

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> Sir F.W. Hutton lent his weight to the argument when he argued that the first shoals consisted of the young of the inanga followed by the young of the smelt and then mixed shoals of kokopu and grayling. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,7</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> In former times, there were large shoals of grayling, upokoroa, a fish which now seems to be extinct. <i>(1989,Hold This Land,9</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Finished making a kupanga, or net, which is about fifty feet by four. took a draught of about fifty good-sized fish with it, called the upokororo, or fresh-water herring. [fn] upokororo, grayling. <i>(1847,Great Journey,25</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Silver eels attaining a goodly size are found in every stream; a sort of grayling is taken in a few of the clearest rivers; and the spotted Kokopu, called trout, ... abounds in every brook. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Some miles back [from Kanieri] there is a lovely lake ... its deep water ... is full of a kind of grayling, ... <i>(Feb 5, 1868,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Feb 5 Harper Letters from Canterbury (1914) 120]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 91. UPOKORORO. ... The above is the native name of the Grayling (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>), a fish that has been long familiar to settlers in certain districts, but ... remained undescribed till last year ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,123</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 91. PROTOTROCTES OXYRHYNCUS. Gunth. Grayling. <i>(1872,Fishes,57</i>)

>  <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>), Gunther. Upokororo - The Grayling. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/171</i>) [NOTES: (Arthur)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> GRAYLING (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>)). ... Called by the Natives upokororo. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,135</i>)

>  The following is a list of the fishes which are chiefly met with in the market: hapuku, kahawai, red-snapper, snapper, tarakihi, moki, trumpeter, frostfish ... gurnard, mullet, sea-mullet, ... flounder or patiki, ... grayling, smelt, kokopu, sardine, sprat, eel (tuna), black-eel, conger-eel [168], and silver-eel. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <i>Upokororo, ... prototroctes oxyrhyncus</i>, grayling, a fish. [so 1917/549; 1957/468 ...freshwater fish] <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,219</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Grayling, n.</i> The Australian fish of that name is <i>Prototroctes marana</i> Gunth. <i>is called also the Fresh-water Herring, Yarra Herring `</i>Melbourne), <i>Cucumber-fish</i>, and <i>Cucumber-Mullet</i>. The last two names are given to it from its smell. It closely resembles the English Grayling. <i>(1898,Austral English,174</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,93</i>)

>  ... <i>Retropinna</i>, or `inanga' of the Maoris, and `smelt' of the settlers; and (2) by the `grayling' of the settlers, or `upokororo', of the Maoris (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>)). <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/312</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A kind of grayling (upokororo) was found abundantly in some rivers but its habitat was limited. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,108</i>)

>  PROTOTROCTES OXYRHYNCHUS Gunther. <i>Grayling; Upokororo</i>. Still to be found in a number of streams in the west coast of the South Island. Highly esteemed as a food fish by Maoris and settlers. An example has recently been taken from a stream in Taranaki. ... Distribution: New Zealand rivers and streams. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) Guenther. GRAYLING; UPOKORORO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,14,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> It consisted of the grayling (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>)), found mostly in clear rapid rivers, <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants,187</i>)

>  The upokororo (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) Guenther), New Zealand grayling, was once very plentiful in the upper branches of the Waiapu, Awatere, Wharekahika, and other streams. <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/636</i>) [NOTES: Buck]

>  <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>): [European Name] Grayling [Maori Name] Upokororo . <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> Grayling, too, must have come into the bill of fare [of thecanterbury moa-hunters]; but this fish ... disappeared so quickly that the <i>upokororo</i> is only a far-off name to most of the elderly Maoris of to-day <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> The Upokororo or Grayling is another [of the Salmonidae], but this fish is almost extinct. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,158</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> GRAYLING OR UPOKORORO <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) Gunther ... This remarkable fish is now rarely seen. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,46</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Upokororo - the grayling. It is mentioned only once. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Another form [of net] was used to catch grayling (<i>upokororo, Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) in the upper reaches of the Waiapu ... and other rivers on the East Coast. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,215</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Grayling or Upokororo <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) Gunther (Syn. <i>Retropinna upokororo</i> Hector) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,400</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 400]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Grayling .. .. kanaekura, paneroro, pokororo, upokororo .. .. <i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>) <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The <i>`grayling' Prototroctes</i> ... was called upokororo by the Maori. ... Just why the upokororo (no relation to the English grayling) has vanished is not known. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,208</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The <i>`grayling' Prototroctes</i> ... was called upokororo by the Maori. ... Just why the upokororo (no relation to the English grayling) has vanished is not known. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,208</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,113</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The upokororo (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>)), a native grayling of somewhat erratic habits, is to be found in some of the rivers, and at certain times of the year afords good sport. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,431</i>)

>  The only true representative of the <i>Salmonidae</i> is the beautiful little <i>Retropinna</i>, or smelt, two varieties of which at least are found in our rivers and lakes. Somewhat allied to this fish, the graceful upokororo (<i>Prototroctes oxyrhynchus</i>), or native grayling ... is found in many of our streams. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/243</i>)

### graze off
 
 <i>v.</i> graze stock on a runoff or separate property away from the main property.

>  It is unprofitable to graze-off drystock when pasture on the home farm is being poorly utilised. <i>(Sep 1997,Dairy Exporter (NZ),29</i>)

>  If a dairy farmer has the facilities, in labour and shed capacity to handle more cows, the finance to get these cows, and is fully stocked up, it will pay him to graze his heifers off. 1977 NZ Farmer Jan 13. 5 The policies of grazing young stock off and buying in hay seem sensible considering the size of the farm. <i>(Apr 30, 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>) [NOTES: (nb: date originally entered into the databasec was 31/4/1968)]

>  The farmer stands to gain if he grazes off dairy heifers from May to May. <i>(Dec 1988,Dairy Exporter (NZ),28</i>)

>  A closer examination will show that unless a ridiculously early culling and drying-off regime is adopted, or lots of stock are grazed-off ... <i>(Oct 1999,Dairy Exporter (NZ),94</i>)

>  This season the heifers are grazed off with 20 more cows projected production 90,000kg m/s. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  Stock going to the saleyards or being grazed off must also carry a secondary tag. 2002 Daily News April 6. 28 This represents approximately 1200 kgs ms/ha on a grass only system where the cows are on the farm all year with the majority of young stock grazed off. [graze-off] <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> graze pasture lightly so that it will recover growth quickly.

>  .. such pastures not only grow a great deal more feed, but recover more rapidly after grazing off and come away earlier in the spring .. it should be possible to start grazing off the spelled paddocks and carry the whole herd through the rest of the winter on grass alone. The more forward paddocks should be grazed off first .. the whole of the area originally spelled will have been grazed off by mid-August <i>(Jan 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,61</i>)

### graze on
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to hold animals on a particular property for a lengthy time. (See [graze-on](go-gra.md#graze-on))

>  IHC also runs a suplementary scheme where calves are grazed on for another two or three years ... <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### graze out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to feed off; to graze completely to the roots of the growth.

>  During early April at least two-thirds of the paddocks on the farm should be successively grazed out, harrowed, and thereafter completely spelled from grazing until mid-June. <i>(Jan 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,61</i>)

>  The paddocks are grazed out systematically and then closed up. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,35</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place stock out for grazing.

>  One of the ways he is doing this [maintaining stock numbers] is by grazing as many cows off the property as possible .. Up to 400 head will be grazed out. <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  Others [farmers] want to close an extra paddock for white clover and are only too happy to graze out their ewes. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,47</i>)

### graze the long acre
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [long acre](liv-lyn.md#long-acre))

### graze-on
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a system of grazing out young calves for priming.

>  Cow numbers required are between 200 and 900 with some positions being graze on. <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

>  Colin is also seeking support for a graze-on scheme, a plan designed to offset the negative effects of dairy farm amalgamation. Using the graze-on scheme, we can sell the same animal for $900, albeit in two years' time. <i>(Oct 22, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a calf grazed to prime for payment

>  <b>IHC</b> We have had a total of 186 graze-ons. <i>(2001,Fundraising News,7</i>)

### grazer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal brought in (hired) to graze surplus pasture.

>  <b>BELL</b> With no money to buy cattle, one had to rely on what 'grazers' were available. <i>(1953,Roundaway: Story of a Sheep Farm,32</i>)

>  Fattening and grazing farm carrying 3000SU in ewes, bulls and dairy grazers. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

>  Then we're ready for the rest of the day feeding out to the beef cows, breakfeeding the grazers, then docking and back to the shed about 4:30. We bought the farm over the road on a high market and the following year everything crashed so if we hadn't had the dairy herd and the grazers ... <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Wanted: Part-time or fulltime labour unit for extensive dry stock unit running sheep, beef and dairy grazers. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

### grazier
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a landowner who leases land or ''takes in'' stock for grazing [particularly dairy cows or dairy beef.]

>  The grazier has no lien over the stock for grazing fees ... <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,58</i>)

>  Grazing available from early April for dairy replacements any age from weaning until calving, also dairy cows to winter, by experienced graziers and dairy farmers. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

>  Grazier should preferably be easily contactable and on hand for at least some week days. <i>(May 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)

>  She knew of at least two disputes that were heading to court after farmers refused to pay graziers because stock had died while away grazing because of lack of feed. <i>(Nov 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> someone who supplies grazing

>  GRAZING / AVAILABLE FROM / EARLY APRIL / for dairy replacement / any age from weaning / until calving, also / dairy cows to winter / by experienced / graziers and dairy / farmers. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

>  [Advt] Grazing needs to be very close to town. Grazier should preferably be easily contactable and on hand for at least some week days. <i>(May 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. pastoralist](p-per.md#pastoralist))

>  We are so far in the country now, and consequently unable to procure fresh meat from graziers ... that we have to cater for ourselves in the way of meat, which involves some pig-hunting. <i>(Feb 17, 1850,Torlesse Papers,132,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [pre-Cby Settlement]]

>  <b>FITTON</b> It may not ... be very clear ... how sheep-keeping in the colonies becomes generally so profitable to graziers. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),219</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> No one can have traversed ... the rich open valleys that invite the grazier in many parts of the North Island, without being struck with their peculiar fitness for stock-farming. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,3</i>)

>  I am now a confirmed grazier; little enough, is it not, for all one's high aspirations to end in? <i>(Aug 8, 1861,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Aug 8 Butler to Sir W.T.Marriott in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/97]

>  Very large purchases are now made in this way from the freezing companies in New Zealand, who are thus enabled to fix the prices which they can pay to graziers ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,314</i>)

>  ... at such times there is a good deal of notice given to the subject of `irrigation by farmers and graziers'. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,156</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The small run grazier has, like the large pastoralist, often to contend with distance from the market ... <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,30</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Cocksfoot is the sheet anchor of the New Zealand grazier ... <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,113</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> The lease expired in 1882, when government subdivided the property and released it to small graziers. <i>(1930,Sketch of His Life,88</i>)

>  The exceptions I mentioned are the graziers in the Karaka district who got into cropping at the instigation of the green-pea canners. <i>(Dec 1952,Here & Now,50</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> It was easy to see what induced graziers to bring sheep here for the summer grazing ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,167,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> A cadaverous man came out and went noisily downstairs, passing Polson with a desultory nod. Nathan Thomas, the grazier. <i>Ibid.</i> 31 Talking to a grazier about the land question, he had used the phrase `White Squattocracy'. He had said it casually and included himself in his usual derogatory way. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoralist who owned sheep or cattle and leased land or owned large tracts of land.

>  The extensive pastoral farms supply the half-bred, Corriedale, three-quarter-bred, and Romney ewes to the intensive graziers and arable mixed farmers on the plains for breeding fat lambs for export. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,119</i>)

>  Graziers and dairymen are quite well satisfied with the present state of things. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,291</i>)

>  A few fortunate graziers, who have sufficient feed left, compete with the butchers for the 'half-fats' which they can buy at about ordinary prices for stores. <i>(Sep 1889,NZ Farmer,359</i>)

>  Unfinished lots sold well, being mostly bought by graziers. <i>(Mar 9, 1907,Farmers' Union Advocate,8</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Frequently the surplus grass runs to seed and afterwards there is a great deal of what graziers call 'flag.' <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,22</i>)

### grazing
 
 <i>adj.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] place of work or residence.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> How many sheep they'd lost that winter I don't know as I'd changed my grazing before the next shearing muster. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,29</i>)

### grazing block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a runoff, where stock are grazed for a limited period with little attention.

>  Approx 60% flat land, ideal runoff or grazing block, one hayshed. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

### grazing farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a drystock property.

>  We currently have this large dairy heifer grazing farm for sale... <i>(Dec 11, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,37</i>)

### grazing licence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a licence to occupy land with stock.

>  <b>ROBERTS & TURNER</b> They set fire to much scrub country before returning to apply for sheep-grazing licences. <i>(1983,NZ High Country Four Seasons,41</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> On his return he applied for a grazing licence of approximately 16,000 acres lying between the TukiTuki and Waipawa Rivers. <i>(1975,Pioneer Trails of Hawke's Bay,29</i>)

>  Write to the Lands Minister .. asking for more funding for whole property purchase, greater recognition of conservation values in tenure review proposals, and for grazing licences to not be issued over large ares of new conservation land. <i>(May 2002,Conservation News,1</i>)

### grazing month
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [pasture management] a unit of measure of natural feed available over a period of time.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> .. some 10,350 grazing months were obtained from the summer country. For a sheep flock of 8,600, this is 10 per cent of all grazing months required. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,6</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The 900 grazing months still shown on the retirement plan country in summer and winter is a small mob of wethers ... <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,24</i>)

### grazing on
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the grazing of stock on a separate property. (See [graze-on](go-gra.md#graze-on))

>  Word among farmers at the sale was many of the hoggets would be heading south for grazing on or immediate slaughter. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,38</i>)
